## 1. Background information on the procedure

Pursuant to Article 16 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 1234/2008, Eli Lilly Nederland B.V. submitted to the European Medicines Agency on 06 November 2025 an application for a variation.

The following changes were proposed:

| Variation(s) requested   | Variation(s) requested                                                              | Type              |
|--------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------|
| C.I.6.a                  | C.I.6.a Addition of a new therapeutic indication or modification of an approved one | Variation type II |

Extension of indication to reduce the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction, or stroke) in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus and established cardiovascular disease for MOUNJARO, based on final results from study I8F-MC-GPGN (SURPASSCVOT). SURPASS-CVOT was a Phase 3, event-driven, multicentre, international, randomised, doubleblind, active-comparator, parallel-group study to assess the effect of tirzepatide versus dulaglutide on major adverse cardiovascular events in participants with type 2 diabetes. As a consequence, sections 4.1, 4.4, 4.8 and 5.1 of the SmPC are updated. The Package Leaflet is updated in accordance. Version 8.1 of the RMP has also been submitted. In addition, the MAH took the opportunity to introduce minor editorial and formatting changes to the PI.

The requested variation(s) proposed amendments to the Summary of Product Characteristics, Annex II and Package Leaflet and to the Risk Management Plan (RMP).

## Information on paediatric requirements

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 1901/2006, the application included (an) EMA Decision(s) EMA/PE/0000237950 on the agreement of a paediatric investigation plan (PIP).

At the time of submission of the application, the PIP EMA/PE/0000237950 was completed.

## Information relating to orphan market exclusivity

## Similarity

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No. 141/2000 and Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 847/2000, the MAH did not submit a critical report addressing the possible similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products because there is no authorised orphan medicinal product for a condition related to the proposed indication.

SURPASS-CVOT was a Phase 3, event-driven, multicentre, international, randomised, double-blind, active-comparator, parallel-group study to assess the effect of tirzepatide versus dulaglutide on major adverse cardiovascular events in participants with type 2 diabetes. As a consequence, sections 4.1, 4.4, 4.8 and 5.1 of the SmPC are updated. The Package Leaflet is updated in accordance. Version 10.1 of the RMP has also been submitted. In addition, the MAH took the opportunity to introduce minor editorial and formatting changes to the PI.

## 4. Scientific discussion

## 4.1. Introduction

## 4.1.1. Problem statement

The MAH initially requested an extension of indication to include the reduction of the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events. The following indication was initially proposed to be added to section 4.1 of the SmPC: \"Mounjaro is indicated to reduce the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction, or stroke) in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus and established cardiovascular disease.\"

Following the assessment of all data provided, the CHMP concluded that a separate indication is not acceptable. The CHMP does, however, support the addition of relevant data of the CVOT trial in section 5.1 of the SmPC, and a reference to these data in SmPC section 4.1.

The final diabetes indication, as accepted by CHMP, is as follows (new text in bold):

\"Type 2 diabetes mellitus

Mounjaro is indicated for the treatment of adults, adolescents and children aged 10 years and above with insufficiently controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus as an adjunct to diet and exercise

- as monotherapy when metformin is considered inappropriate due to intolerance or contraindications
- in addition to other medicinal products for the treatment of diabetes.

For study results with respect to combinations, effects on glycaemic control, the populations studied, and cardiovascular events see sections 4.4, 4.5 and 5.1.\"

The efficacy data supporting the new indication application is derived from the Phase 3 trial I8F-MC GPGN (SURPASS-CVOT). SURPASS-CVOT was a phase 3, event-driven, multicentre, international, randomised, double-blind, active-comparator, parallel-group study. The study assessed the effect of QW tirzepatide maximum tolerated dose up to 15 mg on MACE (cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction or stroke) compared with QW dulaglutide 1.5 mg, when added to the standard of care in participants with type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2D) and established cardiovascular disease. SURPASSCVOT and SUPRASS-CVOT diabetic retinopathy (DR) addendum provide the safety data to support this application, in addition to data submitted earlier in the initial T2D and the weight management applications. The SURPASS-CVOT DR addendum was a sub-study to investigate DR progression with tirzepatide treatment compared to dulaglutide.

## Disease or condition

T2D is a metabolic condition characterized by impaired glycaemic control resulting from increased insulin resistance and progressive beta-cell dysfunction, leading to insufficient insulin secretion. According to the International Diabetes Federation (IDF 2025), an estimated 589 million adults globally are currently living with diabetes, a number projected to rise to 853 million by 2050. T2D is commonly associated with comorbidities like increased weight or obesity, hypertension, and dyslipidaemia, which contribute to an elevated risk of both macrovascular and microvascular complications. T2D is responsible for an average 2-fold higher risk for CV events, including coronary heart disease, ischemic stroke, heart failure event requiring hospitalisation and/or urgent heart failure visit (HFE), and vascular death, independent of other established risk factors (Rawshani et al. 2018; Martin et al. 2025). Approximately 90% of patients with T2D with obesity or overweight, and excess weight plays a central role in the development of insulin resistance and related cardiometabolic complications. CVD, particularly ASCVD, remains the leading cause of morbidity and mortality in people with diabetes, especially those with T2D (Rawshani et al. 2017). This elevated risk is largely attributable to the frequent clustering of major CV risk factors in T2D, including hypertension, dyslipidaemia, and obesity. Treatment of T2D in patients with ASCVD The overarching treatment goals for individuals with T2D and ASCVD include the prevention of macrovascular complications such as MACE, as well as microvascular complications, including diabetic kidney disease, retinopathy, and neuropathy (de Boer et al. 2022). Management of these patients is multifactorial, including lifestyle modification, glycaemic control, reduction of elevated BP, lipoproteins, and weight. Current international guidelines emphasize the use of SGLT-2is and GLP-1 RAs in patients with T2D who have ASCVD, high CV risk, CKD, or HF (Davies et al. 2022). These drug classes offer cardiorenal protection beyond glucose lowering, with evidence supporting their role in reducing CV and kidney-related adverse outcomes. Furthermore, both SGLT-2is and GLP-1 RAs have been shown to significantly lower the burden of CVD and improve long-term outcomes in patients with T2D (ADA 2025). Since 2008, glucose-lowering agents such as SGLT-2is and GLP-1 RAs have been approved for CVD risk reduction. A series of placebo-controlled CVOTs have been conducted in patients with T2D and high CV risk (Bosco et. al. 2021). These trials evaluated MACE as a composite primary endpoint, including MACE-3: CV death, MI, and stroke, MACE-4: MACE-3 plus hospitalization for unstable angina or coronary revascularization, and MACE-5: MACE-4 plus hospitalization due to HF. Recent evidence indicates that the metabolic effects of GLP-1 RAs can be enhanced by combining them with other enteropancreatic hormones, such as GIP RAs and/or glucagon RAs. The combined action of GLP-1 and GIP RAs has been associated with variable improvements in glucose and lipid homeostasis, along with favourable effects on body weight, BP, and inflammatory markers. As a result, tirzepatide may also offer cardioprotective benefits for patients with T2D (Drucker and Holst 2023).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Patient burden and unmet medical need: a significant portion of patients with ASCVD and T2D do not reach treatment targets and have increased risk for further vascular complications (Rawshani et al. 2018). Patients could benefit from new treatment options demonstrating superior glycaemic control combined with other favourable changes in cardiometabolic risk indicators and prevention of MACE. However, as GLP-1 receptor agonists (semaglutide, liraglutide, dulaglutide) have shown to be cardioprotective, the unmet need is partially alleviated. .

## 4.1.2. About the product

## Pharmacological class of tirzepatide

Tirzepatide (TZP) is a long-acting dual glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide (GIP) receptor and glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1) receptor agonist. TZP includes a 39-amino acid peptide sequence and a C20 fatty acid moiety that is covalently linked to Lys20 via a linker with 3 additional amino acids (a γGlu and two 8-amino-3,6-dioxaoctanoic acids) for a total of 42 amino acids. The C20 fatty diacid moiety enables albumin binding and prolongs the half-life. TZP selectively binds to and activates both the GIP and GLP-1 receptors, the targets for native GIP and GLP-1. The molecular weight of TZP is 4.8 kDa.

## Mode of action of tirzepatide

In preclinical studies, tirzepatide showed an equal affinity for the GIP receptors compared with native GIP but was bound with the GLP-1 receptors with approximately a 5-fold weaker affinity than native GLP-1. The dual signalling triggered by tirzepatide results in body weight reduction and improved carbohydrate and lipid metabolism as well as a reduction in blood pressure.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Approved indications and doses

Tirzepatide was first authorized to be marketed on 13 May 2022 in the United States and since has been approved for one or more of the following indications in more than 65 countries, including in the EU, Canada, Australia, Japan, Switzerland, and China, with applications either approved or under review in other regions:

-T2D in adults to improve glycaemic control

- weight management in adults with obesity, or overweight with weight-related comorbidities, and
- moderate-to-severe obstructive sleep apnoea in adults with obesity.

Six tirzepatide dose strengths (2.5, 5, 7.5, 10, 12.5, and 15 mg) are available. The starting dose of tirzepatide is 2.5 mg once weekly. After 4 weeks, the dose should be increased to 5 mg once weekly. The dosage may be increased in 2.5-mg increments after at least 4 weeks on the current dose. The maximum dose of tirzepatide in adults is 15 mg once weekly.

Dose selection for SURPASS-CVOT evaluated tirzepatide MTD up to 15 mg subcutaneous once weekly. Participants were allowed to be treated with lower doses of tirzepatide if clinically indicated. These doses were chosen primarily based on overall CV safety, efficacy (glycaemic control and weight loss), and GI tolerability data (Del Prato et al. 2021; Sattar et al. 2022).

## 4.1.3. The development programme/compliance with CHMP guidance/scientific advice

No EMA Scientific Advice was sought prior to submission. The Application was generally in accordance with applicable EMA Guidance.

## 4.1.4. General comments on compliance with good clinical practice (GCP)

The study supporting this application was conducted under the supervision of an independent review board, with adequate informed consent procedures, and in accordance with consensus ethics principles derived from international ethics guidelines, including the Declaration of Helsinki and the Council for International Organizations of Medical Sciences, the International Council for Harmonization GCP Guideline [E6], and applicable laws and regulations. Clinical studies conducted outside of the EU met the ethical requirements of Directive 2001/20/EC. Studies conducted outside of the US met the conditions of 21 Code of Federal Regulations 312.120. Clinical endpoints were adjudicated by an independent clinical endpoint committee (CEC) and safety was monitored by an independent data monitoring committee.

## 4.2.4. Ecotoxicity/environmental risk assessment (ERA)

The ERA provided is considered complete and acceptable. Tirzepatide is administered in parenteral form and has been described to be not excreted in unchanged form (m1.6, m2.5, m5.3). Tirzepatide includes a 39-amino acid peptide sequence and a 1,20-eicosanedioc acid moiety that is covalently linked to Lys20 via a linker with 3 additional amino acids (a γ-Glu and two 8-amino-3,6-dioxaoctanoic acids) for a total of 42 amino acids. The 1,20-eicosanedioic acid moiety is identical to naturally occurring substances. The fate of the linker is not known. The MAH has provided evidence that tirzepatide is readily biodegradable.

Tirzepatide is not expected to pose a risk to the environment.

## 4.2.5. Discussion on non-clinical aspects

The active substance is a protein covalently linked with a 20-carbon fatty acid, excretion of the intact molecule from humans does not occur. Therefore, tirzepatide is not expected to pose a risk to the environment. There are no open issues left in the ERA. The ERA provided is considered complete and acceptable. No ERA studies are required with respect to the chemical nature of the molecule.

## 4.2.6. Conclusion on the non-clinical aspects

N/A

During assessment no issue of GCP non-compliance arose.

## 4.4.2. Main study

## Title of Study

The Effect of Tirzepatide versus Dulaglutide on Major Adverse Cardiovascular Events in Participants with Type 2 Diabetes (SURPASS-CVOT; I8F-MC-GPGN); Study Initiation Date: 29 May 2020, first participant first visit; Study Completion Date: 12 June 2025, last participant last visit. The analyses presented in this report are based on a database lock date of 24 July 2025.

Regulatory Agency Identifier Number, IND: 128801,EudraCT number: 2019-002735-28, EU trial number: 2023-507846-96-00.

## Methods

## Design

Study GPGN (SURPASS-CVOT) was a phase 3, event-driven, multicenter, international, randomised, double-blind, active comparator, parallel-group study. The study assessed the effect of tirzepatide maximum tolerated dose (MTD; up to 15 mg) versus dulaglutide (1.5 mg), both administered once weekly, on CV outcomes when added to the standard of care in participants with type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2D) with established cardiovascular disease (CVD). Eligible participants were randomised 1:1 to either tirzepatide MTD (up to 15 mg) or dulaglutide 1.5 mg injected subcutaneously QW. CV outcomes were assessed as they occurred, and other measures were collected every 4 weeks for the first 6 months and then followed approximately every 3 months thereafter.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Randomisation was stratified by country of enrollment and SGLT-2i use (Yes/No) at baseline.

The starting dose of tirzepatide was 2.5 mg QW, which was escalated at 4-week intervals to a maximum of 15 mg QW or to the highest maintenance dose tolerated by the participant. Patients were allowed to be on lower maintenance doses of tirzepatide (5 mg or 10 mg) due to GI intolerance. Patients on lower maintenance doses than 15 mg prior to Visit 16 underwent a second dose escalation in an attempt to reach 15 mg. Dulaglutide was initiated and maintained at 1.5 mg QW. To maintain blinding, a sham escalation of dulaglutide was employed.

A total of 1663 participants reported at least 1 CEC-confirmed MACE-3 event. The median follow-up time was 210.1 weeks.

To reduce the risk of hypoglycaemia, individually-tailored adjustments of respective antihyperglycaemic medications were considered beginning with initiation of study drug at Week 0 and throughout the entire study.

The study consisted of the following periods

- ·Screening: 2 weeks
- ·Dose-escalation period: 24 weeks, and

·Maintenance (follow-up) period: continued until at least 1615 CEC-confirmed MACE-3 events occurred.

A total of 1663 participants reported at least 1 CEC-confirmed MACE-3 event. The median follow-up time was 210.1 weeks (=actual duration of the study).

An overview of the design is given in the following figure:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

<!-- image -->

Abbreviation: QW = once weekly.

Figure 1 - Study design for SURPASS-CVOT

## Study participants

This study was conducted at 650 sites that screened 16,979 participants, and randomly assigned 13,299 participants at 640 sites in 30 countries in North America, South America, Europe, and the Asia-Pacific regions.

## Main in- and exclusion criteria

Participants were eligible to be included in the study if they

- were at least 40 years old with a diagnosis of T2D
- had established CVD, including at least 1 of the following:
- -coronary artery disease,
- -cerebrovascular disease,
- -peripheral arterial disease.
- had haemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) ≥7.0% (≥53 mmol/mol) and ≤10.5% (≤91 mmol/mol) based on central laboratory assessment at screening, and
- had body mass index (BMI) ≥25 kg/m2.

Participants were not eligible for this study if they

- had type 1 diabetes mellitus
- were planning treatment for diabetic retinopathy, macular oedema, or both
- had been hospitalized for congestive heart failure within 2 months prior to screening
- were planning a coronary, carotid, or peripheral artery revascularization

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- had a history of chronic or acute pancreatitis
- had a known clinically significant gastric emptying abnormality such as severe gastroparesis or gastric outlet obstruction, or had undergone or were planning any gastric bypass (bariatric) surgery or restrictive bariatric surgery
- had acute or chronic hepatitis, signs or symptoms of any other liver disease, an alanine aminotransferase (ALT) level ≥3× the upper limit of normal (ULN) for the reference range, as determined by the central laboratory
- had known chronic severe renal failure (eGFR (eGFR &lt;15 mL/minute/1.73 m2) or were on chronic dialysis; and
- had family or personal history of multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2 or familial medullary thyroid carcinoma (MTC), or personal history of nonfamilial MTC.

## Treatments

Participants were randomly assigned to tirzepatide or matching dulaglutide 1.5 mg, administered subcutaneously QW via a single-dose pen.

Patient reported outcome measures

## Ability to Perform Physical Activities of Daily Living

Brief description: the APPADL questionnaire contains 7 items that assess how difficult it is for participants to engage in certain activities considered to be integral to normal daily life, such as walking, standing, and climbing stairs. Items were scored on a 5-point numeric rating scale ranging from 1 \"unable to do\" to 5 \"not at all difficult.\" Raw total scores were derived by summing the item scores and dividing by the number of items. Scores were then linearly transformed to a range from 0 to 100. Higher raw APPADL total scores and higher transformed APPADL total scores correspond to better self-reported ability to perform physical activities of daily living.

## Impact of Weight on Self Perception (IW-SP questionnaire)

Brief description: the IW-SP questionnaire contains 3 items that assess how often the participants' body weight affects how happy they are with their appearance and how often they feel self-conscious when out in public. Each item was rated on a 5-point scale ranging from 1 \"always\" to 5 \"never.\" Raw scores were derived by summing the item scores and dividing by the number of items. Scores were then linearly transformed to a range from 0 to 100. Higher IW-SP raw scores and higher transformed scores correspond to better self-perception.

## EQ-5D-5L

Brief description: the EQ-5D-5L (EuroQoL Group, 2019) is a standardized measure of health status that provides a simple, generic measure of health for clinical and economic appraisal. The EQ-5D-5L consists of 2 components:

- -a descriptive system of the respondent's health, and
- -a rating of his or her current health state using a 0 to 100 mm VAS.

The descriptive system comprises the following 5 dimensions:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- -mobility
- -self-care
- -usual activities
- -pain or discomfort, and
- -anxiety or depression.

The 5L version, introduced in 2005, scores each dimension for a total of 3125 possible health states (also referred to as health profile) at 5 levels:

- -no problems
- -slight problems
- -moderate problems
- -severe problems, and
- -unable to perform or extreme problems.

In addition to the health profile, a single health state index value can be derived based on a countryspecific formula that attaches weights to each of the levels in each dimension. This index value ranges between less than zero (where zero is a health state equivalent to death; negative values are valued as worse than dead) and 1 (perfect health).

The VAS records the respondent's self-rated health status on a vertical VAS whereby the endpoints are labelled as \"the best health you can imagine\" (100) and \"the worst health you can imagine\" (zero). This information can be used as a quantitative measure of health-related quality of life outcome.

## Sample size

This is an event-driven study. The study will be continued until at least 1615 patients experience 1 or more components of the CEC-confirmed MACE-3 endpoint. This sample size, i.e., 1615 patients with MACE-3, provides 90% power to demonstrate the superiority of tirzepatide compared to dulaglutide, relative to MACE-3 risk, at a 2-sided alpha of a 0.0472 significance level if tirzepatide is associated with a 15% reduction in hazard compared to dulaglutide for the MACE-3 endpoint (nQuery + nTerim 4.0 software). The significance level is adjusted to account for an efficacy assessment at an interim analysis. It is assumed that the log-rank test will be used for comparing the distribution of time to first occurrence of MACE-3 between tirzepatide and dulaglutide.

Although the sample size is guided by superiority, the primary study objective is to demonstrate NI of tirzepatide to dulaglutide. An NI boundary of 1.05 will be used, which represents the inverse of the 97.5th percentile of the posterior distribution of the hazard ratio (HR) for dulaglutide versus placebo for the MACE-3 endpoint. The posterior distribution of the HR was derived using a Bayesian, hierarchical meta-analysis of patients with a history of CV disease from 6 completed glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonist (GLP-1 RA) cardiovascular outcomes trials (CVOTs), including the REWIND CVOT (Gerstein et al. 2019). Additionally, the NI margin of 1.05 ensures preservation of at least 50% of the dulaglutide efficacy estimated using the Bayesian, hierarchical meta-analysis. The accrual of 1615 patients with at least 1 component of MACE-3 provides 90% power to establish NI of tirzepatide to dulaglutide (the upper bound of the 95.3% confidence interval [CI] of the HR, tirzepatide versus dulaglutide, is ≤1.05), provided that tirzepatide is associated with a 10.5% reduction in hazard compared to dulaglutide, relative to the MACE-3 endpoint.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Extrapolated on evidence from previous trials (Zinman et al. 2015; Marso et al. 2016; Hernandez et al. 2018; Gerstein et al. 2019), approximately 35 patients are expected to experience the MACE-3 outcome for 1000 patient-years of exposure in the dulaglutide treatment group. Therefore, approximately 12,500 patients enrolled and followed for an average of 4 years will lead to the accrual of at least 1615 primary endpoints. It is assumed that 2% of the potential exposures will not be realized due to discontinuations from the study and deaths unrelated to CV causes.

## Randomisation

Assignment to tirzepatide or dulaglutide was randomly allocated in a 1:1 ratio. Randomisation was stratified by country of enrollment and sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 inhibitor (SGLT-2i) use (Yes/No) at baseline.

## Blinding (masking)

This study was double-blinded. Until the end of the study, treatment assignments remained blinded for the Sponsor, Investigators, site staff, clinical monitors and participants. The identity of tirzepatide and dulaglutide was masked because both study drugs were provided in an identical single-dose pen, and a tirzepatide-matched sham dose-escalation or de-escalation schedule was followed for participants receiving dulaglutide.

## Statistical methods

The final statistical analysis plan (SAP) Version 4 was approved on 24 July 2025 prior to database lock and unblinding on the same day.

## Analysis data sets

The modified intention-to-treat (mITT) dataset consists of data from all randomised participants collected during the follow-up period (date of randomisation to the date of participant's end of followup), excluding data from those participants who were randomised in error in the study. The safety dataset consists of data from all randomised participants who took at least 1 dose of study drug (date of first dose to the date of participant's end of follow-up).

## Efficacy analyses

Unless otherwise specified, efficacy analyses were based on the mITT dataset and were conducted using data collected in the study regardless of study drug status. For selected continuous and categorical measurements, a hybrid imputation approach for missing values at 24 or 36 months was conducted. In addition, 'On Treatment' analyses were conducted for selected parameters, using data collected during the first on-treatment observation period, defined as up to the date of last study drug plus 7 days, with the date of last study drug defined as the earlier of the date the participant permanently discontinued study drug or interrupted study drug for 3 months or longer.

## Interim and final analysis

One interim analysis of the primary endpoint was performed to evaluate early termination of the study due to overwhelming efficacy of tirzepatide versus dulaglutide. The interim analysis was planned to be conducted once approximately 1075 CEC-confirmed MACE-3 endpoints had been accrued and occurred on 05 January 2024 when 1078 participants experienced at least 1 MACE-3 event. Since tirzepatide was not superior to dulaglutide at the interim analysis at alpha level of 0.01, the study continued without modification according to the independent data monitoring committee recommendation. The final analysis occurred when 1663 participants experienced at least 1 primary endpoint of MACE-3.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Primary efficacy analysis

The primary analysis of the primary endpoint was the time from randomisation to first occurrence of clinical endpoint committee (CEC)- confirmed MACE-3 (cardiovascular [CV] death, MI, or stroke) based on the mITT dataset using a Cox proportional hazards model stratified by sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 inhibitor (SGLT-2i) use (yes/no) at baseline with treatment as a fixed effect. Treatment group comparisons were based on the estimates of hazard ratio and 95.3% (significance level of 0.047 after adjustment for interim analysis) confidence interval (CI) calculated from the Cox model stratified by SGLT-2i use at baseline. Tirzepatide was noninferior to dulaglutide if the upper bound from the primary analysis was less than 1.05 and superior if it was less than 1.0. Kaplan-Meier (KM) estimates of the survival curve for each treatment group were generated. The incidence rate per 100 person-years of follow-up was calculated for each treatment group.

## Determination of the non-inferiority margin

An NI margin of 1.05 for comparing tirzepatide and dulaglutide is chosen because

1. represents the inverse of the 97.5th percentile of the posterior distribution of HR (dulaglutide versus placebo) derived using a hierarchical Bayesian analysis based on the secondary prevention cohort from the REWIND CVOT comparing dulaglutide and placebo and secondary prevention cohorts from several GLP-1 RA CVOTs, and
2. an upper bound of a 2-sided 95% CI for the HR (tirzepatide versus dulaglutide) ≤1.05 in a 1615-endpoint CVOT ensures the point estimate of the HR (tirzepatide versus dulaglutide) &lt;0.95; hence, it can be shown using the \"synthesis method\" outlined in the FDA guidance, \"Non-Inferiority Clinical Trials to Establish Effectiveness\" that observed HR &lt;0.95 ensures preservation of at least 50% of the dulaglutide efficacy in REWIND CVOT (FDA 2016).

## Key secondary efficacy analysis

Analyses of selected key secondary MACE efficacy measures were based on the time from randomisation to the occurrence of the first event using the Cox model stratified by SGLT-2i use at baseline. Change from baseline in estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) in participants with highor very-high-risk chronic kidney disease (CKD) was analyzed based on an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) model with treatment, country, and SGLT-2i use at baseline as fixed factors and baseline as a covariate.

## Type 1 error control for primary and key secondary efficacy analysis

Multiplicity-adjusted analyses were planned for the primary and key secondary objectives for testing the superiority of tirzepatide compared with dulaglutide to control the overall family-wise Type I error rate at a 1-sided alpha level of 0.025. This approach strongly controls the Type I error rate across both interim and final analyses and all primary and key secondary objectives.

The testing strategy at the interim and final analysis is illustrated in Figure 2, where each node represents a hypothesis. The primary endpoint MACE-3 noninferiority, was allocated with full alpha initially. Each directed edge was assigned a transition weight between 0 and 1, representing the fraction of the preserved alpha that was transferred from the hypothesis at the head node to the hypothesis at the tail node, provided the hypothesis at the head node was successfully rejected. The alpha-spending approach was applied to each hypothesis using the alpha allocated at each step of the algorithm to control multiplicity at both interim and final analysis.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

<!-- image -->

Abbreviations: CV = cardiovascular; eGFR = estimated glomerular filtration rate; HFE = heart failure (HF) event requiring hospitalization and/or urgent HF visit; MACE-3 = death from cardiovascular or undetermined causes, myocardial infarction, or stroke; MACE-4 = death from cardiovascular or undetermined causes, myocardial infarction, stroke, or coronary revascularization; NI = noninferiority; SUP = superiority.

Figure 2 -Type 1 error control strategy for primary and key secondary efficacy endpoints.

## Imputation of missing data

For the primary endpoint, a sensitivity analysis was performed based on the imputed value. Specifically, missing follow-up time for participants whose primary endpoint status was not ascertained during the close-out period was imputed using an event-rate model estimated for the primary endpoint. For participants who prematurely discontinued the study due to administrative reasons, specifically, those enrolled at a closed site and not transferred to another site, they were assumed to be missing at random. For other participants, the event-rate model was derived from retrieved dropouts, defined as participants who permanently discontinued the study drug for reasons other than death, remained in the study, and did not experience the primary endpoint prior to study drug discontinuation, within the same treatment group.

For continuous variables, such as change in eGFR and HbA1c, a landmark time point of 36 months from randomisation was chosen, unless otherwise specified, as most randomly assigned participants were expected to reach this time point. Missing values at the landmark were imputed based on the same consistent assumptions applied in imputing missing follow-up time described above.

## Results

## Participant flow

A total of 13,299 participants were randomly assigned 1:1 to either tirzepatide treatment group or dulaglutide treatment group. Of these, 134 participants were randomly assigned in error and were therefore discontinued early from the study. The remaining 13,165 participants were included in the mITT population. In the mITT population, a total of 13,029 participants (tirzepatide, 6524 [99.1%]; dulaglutide, 6505 [98.9%]) completed the study in the following category: experienced a MACE-3 endpoint: 1663 (tirzepatide, 801 [12.2%]; dulaglutide, 862 [13.1%]), non-CV death: 403 (tirzepatide, 175 [2.7%]; dulaglutide, 228 [3.5%]), and completed end-of-study visit without occurrence of MACE3: 10,963 (tirzepatide, 5548 [84.2%]; dulaglutide, 5415 [82.3%]).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A total of 136 participants (tirzepatide, 62 [0.9%]; dulaglutide, 74 [1.1%]) did not complete the study. The percentage of study discontinuation was comparable between both treatment groups. \"Adverse event\" was the primary reason for study treatment discontinuation in both treatment groups. A total of 10,440 participants (tirzepatide, 5164 [78.4%]; dulaglutide, 5276 [80.2%]) completed the study on treatment. Vital status (dead or alive) was ascertained in 99.7% of participants. Nearly 80% of participants in both treatment groups completed the study on-treatment.

A total of 2724 participants (20.7%) permanently discontinued study drug (tirzepatide, 1422 [21.6%]; dulaglutide, 1302 [19.8%]; p-value = 0.011). The most common reasons for premature, permanent discontinuation of study drug were

- adverse events (tirzepatide, 874 [13.3%]; dulaglutide, 671 [10.2%]), and
- other (tirzepatide, 464 [7.1%]; dulaglutide, 528 [8.0%]).

## Protocol deviations

A total of 4181 (31.4%) participants had at least 1 important protocol deviation (tirzepatide, 30.9%; dulaglutide, 31.9%). Overall, the percentages of important protocol deviations were comparable across the treatment groups. The most common important protocol deviations were related to study procedure compliance (17.2%) and informed consent (11.3%). Important protocol deviations related to eligibility criteria: a total of 179 participants had important protocol deviations related to eligibility criteria. Of these, 134 participants were considered randomised in error and were prematurely discontinued from the study. The remaining 45 participants remained in the study because:

- subsequent laboratory testing confirmed eligibility,
- lack of source documentation that confirmed the participant was ineligible, or
- participants had a history of proliferative retinopathy or macular oedema or non-proliferative DR requiring acute treatment and met exclusion criterion 11 during GPGN Amendment (b) but were eligible after criterion 11 was updated for GPGN Amendment (c).

## Baseline cardiovascular risk for mITT population

Participants reported the following types of CVD at baseline:

- -coronary artery disease: 65.0%,
- -prior coronary revascularization: 57.2%,
- -prior MI: 47.2%,

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

```
-peripheral arterial disease: 25.3%, -prior lower extremity arterial revascularization: 5.6%, -prior stroke: 19.2%, -prior carotid revascularization: 2.9%, and -prior HF: 20.3%.
```

A high percentage of participants had additional CV risk factors at baseline: 90.2% had hypertension, and 85.8% had dyslipidaemia. Most participants' HbA1c was inadequately controlled (mean [SD] HbA1c: 8.4 [0.9]%). Participants had long-standing diabetes (mean [SD] duration of T2D was 14.7 [8.8] years). 67.8% of participants had T2D for at least 10 years. Most participants had obesity or were overweight (mean [SD] BMI: 32.6 [5.5] kg/m 2 ; 86.0% had a BMI ≥27 kg/m 2 and 63.0% had a BMI &gt;30 kg/m 2 ).

A minority of participants had evidence of renal dysfunction at baseline (22.5% of participants had eGFR &lt;60 mL/min/1.73 m2, 32.0% had microalbuminuria, and 11.5% had macroalbuminuria).

Overall, 30.6% of participants were using SGLT-2i.

Based on fundoscopic exam at baseline, 2.0% had macular oedema, 1.6% had proliferative DR, and 28.7% had non-proliferative DR, with most cases being mild (68.6%). A history or preexisting condition of diabetic neuropathy was reported by 20.1% of participants.

## Concomitant Medications

Antihyperglycaemic medication use at baseline

The most frequently used (≥20%) antihyperglycaemic therapies in both the treatment groups at baseline were

- metformin (tirzepatide group: 81.1%; dulaglutide group: 81.7%),
- insulins (tirzepatide group: 49.4%; dulaglutide group: 48.3%),
- SGLT-2i (tirzepatide group: 30.4%; dulaglutide group: 30.8%), and
- SUs (tirzepatide group: 21.3%; dulaglutide group: 22.0%).

Antihyperglycaemic medication use postbaseline

## SU and insulin

- During the course of the study, a decrease in the percentage of participants using SUs and insulins was observed in the tirzepatide treatment group.
- At Month 36, the tirzepatide group had a significantly lower percentage of participants using SUs and insulins compared with the dulaglutide group:

SUs: tirzepatide group: 15.0%; dulaglutide group: 21.4%, and

Insulins: tirzepatide group: 39.2%; dulaglutide group: 47.8%.

## SGLT-2i

- During the course of the study, an increase in the percentage of participants using SGLT 2i was observed in the dulaglutide treatment group.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- At Month 36, the tirzepatide group had a significantly lower percentage of participants using SGLT-2i (33.8%) compared with the dulaglutide group (41.1%).

## Antihypertensive therapy

The most frequently used antihypertensive medication were:

- -beta blockers (65.2%)
- -ACE inhibitors (39.8%), and
- -angiotensin receptor blockers (40.5%).

## Lipid-lowering therapy

The most frequently used lipid-lowering medications at baseline were statins (85.8%).

## Blood thinner therapy

The most frequently used antiplatelet medications at baseline were acetylsalicylic acid and platelet aggregation inhibitors, excluding heparin (72.6%). The most frequently used anticoagulant was direct factor Xa inhibitors (6.2%).

## Exposure

The median follow-up time in the study was 210.7 weeks for tirzepatide and 210.0 weeks for dulaglutide. The percentage of participants with follow-up time in study of 36 months or greater was similar between the treatment groups (tirzepatide: 90.5%; dulaglutide: 89.5%).

Duration on study treatment excluding treatment interruption was similar in both treatment groups. The median duration of study on-treatment was 203.1 weeks for the tirzepatide group and 204.1 weeks for the dulaglutide group.

At Month 36, 4922 [74.7%] participants in the tirzepatide group and 4983 [75.7%] participants in the dulaglutide group remained on study drug. Of those receiving tirzepatide,

- -867 (17.6%) tirzepatide-treated participants were receiving 5 mg
- -451 (9.2%) tirzepatide-treated participants were receiving 10 mg
- -3580 (72.7%) tirzepatide-treated participants were receiving 15 mg.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Dose Modification

According to the protocol, the dose of study drug could be de-escalated after the initial dose-escalation and at the investigator's discretion, due to a participant's safety-related concern, such as body weight reduction or other chronic adverse condition (for example, chronic or recurrent GI AEs). Participants on 10 mg of tirzepatide were de-escalated to 5 mg. Participants on 15 mg of tirzepatide were deescalated to 10 mg, or if deemed necessary, to 5 mg. Participants on 5 mg had study drug temporarily interrupted. Participants in the dulaglutide group followed a sham dose de-escalation schedule to match the tirzepatide group's dose de-escalation, but the 1.5 mg dose was maintained, as this represents the sham dose de-escalation to maintain the blinding. Participants who had de-escalated their dose were offered an opportunity to re-escalate when the investigator deemed it appropriate to reinitiate dose-escalation. Participants who did not reach the maximum dose during the initial doseescalation for any reason, had de-escalated their dose after the initial dose-escalation, or both were offered the opportunity to undergo additional attempts at dose-escalation to reach the 15-mg maintenance dose. Whenever study drug was temporarily interrupted, every effort was made to promptly restart as soon as it was safe to do so.

## Outcomes and estimation

## Primary endpoint MACE-3

The primary composite outcome occurred in 801 (12.2%) participants (3.25 events per 100 PY of follow-up) in the tirzepatide group and 862 (13.1%) participants (3.54 events per 100 PY of follow-up) in the dulaglutide group.

Noninferiority of MACE-3 was confirmed for tirzepatide compared to dulaglutide with an HR of 0.92 (95.3% CI: 0.83, 1.01; noninferiority p-value = 0.007). The upper bound of CI (1.01) was less than the prespecified noninferiority margin of 1.05. Superiority of tirzepatide compared to dulaglutide was not achieved (p-value = 0.086).

## Total (first and recurrent) MACE-3

A lower number of total MACE-3 outcomes were reported in the tirzepatide (n=925) group compared with dulaglutide (n=1005) group. The rate ratio for total events was not significantly different in the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group.

Of the total participants who had MACE-3 outcomes:

- 696 participants in the tirzepatide group and 748 in the dulaglutide group each had 1 event,
- 91 participants in the tirzepatide group and 92 in the dulaglutide group each had 2 events,
- 12 participants in the tirzepatide group and 16 in the dulaglutide group each had 3 events, and
- 2 participants in the tirzepatide group and 6 participants in the dulaglutide group each had 4 or more events.

## Sensitivity Analysis of Primary Endpoint

## Imputation of missing follow-up time

In the mITT dataset, the missing follow-up time for 136 participants for whom the primary endpoint status was not ascertained during the close-out period was imputed by the estimated event-rate model for the primary endpoint (retrieved dropouts: a piecewise exponential model with intervals: [0, 0.5], [0.5, 1], [1, infinity] years; missing at random: an exponential model). Across 100 imputed datasets for the mITT participants, the primary endpoint occurred in a mean of 808 participants (12.3%) in the tirzepatide group and 870 participants (13.2%) in the dulaglutide group with an estimated HR of 0.92 (95.3% CI 0.83 to 1.01; p-value = 0.079). Based on the simulation results, on average, 7 tirzepatide and 8 dulaglutide-treated participants of 136 participants (tirzepatide: 62: dulaglutide 74) whose primary endpoint status was not ascertained during the close-out period would have experienced a primary endpoint if they had been followed up until the end of the study.

## Tipping point analysis

The purpose of the tipping point analysis was to estimate the level of increase needed in the estimated latent event-rate during the missing follow-up time from the end of follow-up to the end of the study for tirzepatide-treated participants lost to follow-up to reverse the noninferiority conclusion of the primary analysis. The tipping point sensitivity analysis demonstrated that if the primary endpoint event rate among the 62 tirzepatide-treated participants who did not complete the trial had been 9.7 times higher than the estimated event-rate model during their missing follow-up time, the upper bound of the 95.3% CI would have exceeded 1.05, that is, noninferiority would not have been achieved. In real setting, a 9.7-fold increase in event rate would be implausible and implies that the primary analysis result was confirmed.

## On-treatment analysis

An on-treatment sensitivity analysis of the primary endpoint was pre-specified. On-treatment period was defined as up to the date of last study drug plus 30 days, with the date of last study drug defined as the earlier of the participant permanently discontinuing or interrupting study drug for 3 months or longer. The results [hazard ratio (95% CI): 0.91 (0.81, 1.02)] are consistent with the primary analysis, supporting the non-inferiority of tirzepatide compared with dulaglutide.

## Key secondary endpoints

Since superiority of tirzepatide versus dulaglutide was not established, formal statistical comparison between treatment groups of key secondary endpoints was not controlled for family-wise Type 1 error rate. Although 95% CIs and p-values are provided to describe the results, these p-values are nominal.

Key secondary endpoints included time to death due to any cause, time to CV death, time to first occurrence of CV death or HFE, time to first occurrence of expanded composite CV outcome defined as MACE-4, and change from baseline to 36 months in eGFR (CKD-EPI Creatinine-Cystatin Equation 2021) in participants with high- or very-high-risk CKD as defined by KDIGO guideline 2025.

## Time to Death Due to Any Cause

The incidence of all-cause death was nominally significantly lower for the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group. The majority of all-cause deaths occurred due to CV or undetermined causes, with no significant difference between treatment groups.

The most common cause of CV death was sudden cardiac death, with no difference between treatment groups. The most common cause of non-CV deaths was infection, with fewer cases in the tirzepatide group (78 cases with TZP, 138 cases with dulaglutide), followed by malignancies, with no differences between treatment groups. ## Time to First Occurrence of MACE-4 (including coronary revascularisation)

MACE-4 was defined as death due to CV or undetermined cause, MI, stroke, or coronary revascularization. The incidence of MACE-4 was nominally significantly lower for the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group (1089 (16.5) with TZP; 1217 (18.5) with dulaglutide; HR 0.88, CI 0.81, 0.96). The incidence of the MACE-4 component coronary revascularization was nominally significantly lower for the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group (527 (8.0) with TZP, 617 (9.4) with dulaglutide; HR 0.84, CI 0.75, 0.95). This difference was mainly caused by a nominally significantly lower incidence of non-elective revascularisation procedures with TZP as opposed to dulaglutide (no significant difference as regards elective procedures between treatment groups).

## Time to First Occurrence of Hospitalization for Unstable Angina

## Change From Baseline to 36 Months in eGFR in Participants with high- or very-high-risk CKD as defined by KDIGO 2025 criteria

The eGFR was calculated using the CKD-EPI Creatinine-Cystatin Equation 2021.

## Criteria for high-risk or very-high risk CKD population:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

One measurement of eGFR and one measurement of UACR were obtained at baseline, and both values (eGFR and UACR) in combination were used to identify the subpopulation of participants with high- or very-high-risk CKD. Participants with high- or very-high-risk CKD as defined by KDIGO guideline 2025 were identified as having the following eGFR (using the CKD-EPI Creatinine-Cystatin Equation 2021) and UACR thresholds at baseline:

- -eGFR ≥60 mL/min/1.73 m2 and UACR &gt;300 mg/g,
- -eGFR 45 to &lt;60 mL/min/1.73 m2 and UACR &gt;30 mg/g, or
- -eGFR &lt;45 mL/min/1.73 m2.

The following figure and table display change from baseline in eGFR (CKD-EPI Creatinine-Cystatin Equation 2021) to end of follow-up.

## GFR slope

The annual rate of decline in eGFR for participants with high or very-high risk CKD was nominally significantly slower for participants in the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group (0.93 mL/min/1.73m2 per year, 95% CI: 0.65, 1.22, p-value &lt;0.001).

## Subgroup analysis (SGLT-2 inhibitor use at baseline)

At Month 36, the decline in eGFR in participants not using SGLT-2 inhibitor at baseline was significantly reduced for tirzepatide group compared to dulaglutide group (0.9 mL/min/1.73 m 2 , 95% CI: 0.4, 1.5, p-value &lt;0.001). The decline in eGFR in participants using SGLT-2 inhibitor at baseline was not significantly reduced for tirzepatide group compared to dulaglutide group (0.7 mL/min/1.73 m 2 , 95% CI: -0.1, 1.5, p-value = 0.071). The effect size of the hold of eGFR decline was larger in both treatment groups when SGLT2-inhibitors are given concomitantly.

## Role of concomitant SGLT2i therapy

Figure 11 shows the eGFR changes over time based on the 3 different equations (creatinine, cystatin, and creatinine-cystatin based) for participants with high or very high-risk for CKD with or without baseline SGLT-2i use. As prespecified, high or very high-risk CKD is defined as meeting one of the following 3 criteria:

- (1) eGFR &gt;=60 mL/min/1.73m2 and UACR &gt;300 g/kg
- (2) eGFR &gt;=45 mL/min/1.73m2 to &lt;60 and UACR &gt;30 g/kg, or
- (3) eGFR &lt;45 mL/min/1.73m2).

In all instances, when compared with dulaglutide, the progression of eGFR decline was slower on tirzepatide.

These data demonstrate that the clinically meaningful reduced decline in eGFR for tirzepatide compared with dulaglutide is independent of SGLT-2i use.

## Acute positive eGFR slope and impact of weight reduction

There is an initial positive slope in eGFR on tirzepatide preferentially present with eGFR values calculated by the creatinine-based equation. Moreover, this initial positive slope is more apparent in

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

participants on tirzepatide and dulaglutide with baseline SGLT-2i use. The initial eGFR increase in values derived from the pre-specified, guideline-recommended eGFR (creatinine-cystatin) formula, was low in magnitude and observed only in tirzepatide-treated high or very high-risk participants on concomitant SGLT-2i treatment (Figure 12). In all instances, eGFR values start to separate between the 2 treatment groups early, resulting in higher eGFR values in the tirzepatide than in the dulaglutide group.

From the literature, eGFR decrease was less in participants who lost weight in the Look AHEAD study (Zu et al. 2024). Mechanistically, kidney function and protection could benefit from weight-lossassociated lower blood pressure, improved insulin sensitivity, reduced inflammation, decreased albuminuria, lower intrarenal fat accumulation. All these beneficial, potentially kidney protective mechanisms are present in incretin therapies with larger magnitude in tirzepatide than dulaglutide.

The applicant has analysed the correlation between 1-year weight and initial, 1-year eGFR (creatinine-cystatin) change (covering for the initial positive slope) in the high and very high-risk CKD population in the presence or absence of concomitant SGLT-2i treatment at baseline. There are weak

but statistically significant correlations between weight decrease and eGFR

(creatinine-cystatin) increase in both treatment groups in participants not on SGLT-2i at baseline (Figure 13). Similar correlation was present in baseline SGLT-2i users in tirzepatide treatment group but not in dulaglutide treatment group. These associations are weak and may be confounded by numerous concomitant changes with potential kidney benefits. In general, weight loss or weight loss associated changes may affect eGFR but not be the main driver for initial positive eGFR slope.

The analyses of the initial positive slope in the present study have limitations. There are no or only few eGFR measurements earlier than 1 year. From SURPASS-4 in patients with moderate CKD risk, former studies have demonstrated an acute dip in eGFR shortly after initiation (12 weeks), shortly followed by a rebound (Figure 15) (Heerspink et al. 2022). This figure also shows that no drop in eGFR (creatine) was observed after discontinuing treatment suggesting that the preserved eGFR on tirzepatide reflects better kidney status versus change in haemodynamic stage (Figure 15) (Heerspink et al. 2022).

Figure 15 - eGFR (creatinine) change from baseline during treatment and at safety followup in SURPASS-4

<!-- image -->

Abbreviations: CKD-EPI = Chronic Kidney Disease-Epidemiology Collaboration; eGFR = estimated glomerular filtration rate.

As no other substantial evidence was identified explaining the initial positive eGFR slopes, the \"Regression to the mean\" statistical phenomenon subsequent to low eGFR threshold inclusion may play an important role behind the initial positive eGFR slope.

## Potential mechanisms of nephroprotection

The potential nephroprotection by tirzepatide is not fully elucidated. Ongoing mechanism of action study is evaluating different aspects of this phenomenon. The potential larger magnitude of nephroprotection of tirzepatide versus GLP-1 RAs, such as dulaglutide, could be associated with more effective control of traditional nephroprotective actions including

- blood pressure lowering
- weight loss
- reduction of lipoproteins, and
- anti-inflammatory effects.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Clinically relevant long-term changes in eGFR

The initial, transitory increase in eGFR after initiation of tirzepatide in high or very high-risk CKD participants does not explain the differences in eGFR between tirzepatide and dulaglutide treatment groups throughout the study. When considering longer-term changes in eGFR, fewer participants in the tirzepatide arm compared with the dulaglutide arm reached the status of persistent eGFR decline of 40% (HR = 0.70, 95% CI:0.57, 0.85, p&lt;0.001) or 50% (HR=0.73, 95% CI:0.55, 0.96, p Value=0.024) (Table 24) (Zoungas et al. 2026)). These results also demonstrate that the protective effects of tirzepatide on eGFR decline are present in the participants with high or very high risk for CKD, irrespective of initial eGFR changes.

## Systolic blood pressure

Blood pressure measurements were collected at baseline and Weeks 4, 8, 12, 16, 20, 24, and every 6 months thereafter. SBP was significantly decreased from baseline beginning as early as Week 4, at first postbaseline assessment, and was maintained throughout the follow-up in both treatment groups. The maximum decrease in SBP during dose-escalation was reached at Week 24 for tirzepatide (-8.8 mmHg) and at Week 16 for dulaglutide (-4.7 mmHg). The reduction in SBP from baseline to Month 36 was significantly greater in the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group. Significantly greater decreases in SBP in the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group were observed at all time points beginning at Week 8.

## Patient reported outcome measures

Ability to Perform Physical Activities of Daily Living

Brief description: the APPADL questionnaire contains 7 items that assess how difficult it is for participants to engage in certain activities considered to be integral to normal daily life, such as walking, standing, and climbing stairs. Items were scored on a 5-point numeric rating scale ranging from 1 \"unable to do\" to 5 \"not at all difficult.\" Raw total scores were derived by summing the item scores and dividing by the number of items. Scores were then linearly transformed to a range from 0 to 100. Higher raw APPADL total scores and higher transformed APPADL total scores correspond to better self-reported ability to perform physical activities of daily living.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Results: the baseline transformed total APPADL scores for the tirzepatide group were not significantly different compared with the dulaglutide group. Total transformed APPADL scores were nominally significantly improved from baseline to Month 24 in the tirzepatide group, with no significant improvement observed in the dulaglutide group.

Impact of Weight on Self Perception (IW-SP questionnaire)

Brief description: the IW-SP questionnaire contains 3 items that assess how often the participants' body weight affects how happy they are with their appearance and how often they feel self-conscious when out in public. Each item was rated on a 5-point scale ranging from 1 \"always\" to 5 \"never.\" Raw scores were derived by summing the item scores and dividing by the number of items. Scores were then linearly transformed to a range from 0 to 100. Higher IW-SP raw scores and higher transformed scores correspond to better self-perception.

Results: the baseline transformed IW-SP scores for the tirzepatide group were not significantly different compared with dulaglutide. Total transformed IW-SP scores for both treatment groups significantly improved from baseline to Month 24 indicating better self-perception. In the tirzepatide group, an improvement in total transformed IW-SP scores from baseline to Month 24 was observed compared with dulaglutide group.

## EQ-5D-5L

Brief description: the EQ-5D-5L (EuroQoL Group, 2019) is a standardized measure of health status that provides a simple, generic measure of health for clinical and economic appraisal. The EQ-5D-5L consists of 2 components:

- -a descriptive system of the respondent's health, and
- -a rating of his or her current health state using a 0 to 100 mm VAS.

The descriptive system comprises the following 5 dimensions:

- -mobility
- -self-care
- -usual activities
- -pain or discomfort, and
- -anxiety or depression.

The 5L version, introduced in 2005, scores each dimension for a total of 3125 possible health states (also referred to as health profile) at 5 levels:

- -no problems
- -slight problems
- -moderate problems
- -severe problems, and
- -unable to perform or extreme problems.

In addition to the health profile, a single health state index value can be derived based on a countryspecific formula that attaches weights to each of the levels in each dimension. This index value ranges between less than zero (where zero is a health state equivalent to death; negative values are valued as worse than dead) and 1 (perfect health).

The VAS records the respondent's self-rated health status on a vertical VAS whereby the endpoints are labeled as \"the best health you can imagine\" (100) and \"the worst health you can imagine\" (zero). This information can be used as a quantitative measure of health-related quality of life outcome.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Results: baseline Index scores (utilizing the UK algorithm) and EQ VAS scores of the tirzepatide group were not significantly different compared with the dulaglutide group. EQ-5D-5L Index scores for both treatment groups had a significant increase from baseline to Month 24. EQ VAS scores for both treatment groups significantly improved from baseline to Month 24, indicating improved health-related quality of life. Tirzepatide group had no significant treatment difference in change in the EQ-5D-5L Index score from baseline to Month 24 compared with dulaglutide group.

## Ancillary analyses

## Indirect Comparison of Efficacy Results Versus Placebo for the primary endpoint and key secondary endpoints

For the primary analysis of MACE-3, the upper bound of the CI (1.01) was less than the prespecified noninferiority boundary of 1.05. A SAP prespecified indirect comparison of tirzepatide versus placebo was conducted for the primary and CV-related key secondary endpoints utilizing individual participant data from SURPASS-CVOT and REWIND. The analysis included data from REWIND participants who would have been eligible to enroll in SURPASS-CVOT, and all SURPASS-CVOT participants, and estimated the effect of tirzepatide to reduce the CV risk compared with an indirect placebo. In addition, an indirect comparison sensitivity analysis was conducted utilizing all REWIND participants.

For both analyses, the indirect treatment effect was derived by multiplying the HR for SURPASS-CVOT comparing tirzepatide versus dulaglutide by the HR for REWIND comparing dulaglutide versus placebo.

HRs were adjusted based on stabilized inverse-probability weight calculated based on the estimated propensity score. Number needed to treat for MACE-3 was indirectly estimated at the median follow-up of 4 years in SURPASS-CVOT, based on the same manner as the estimation of indirect treatment effect expressed as HR. Adjustment of baseline characteristics in the REWIND population to the SURPASSCVOT population was based on stabilized inverse-probability weight, calculated based on the estimated

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

propensity score, defined as the probability of belonging to SURPASS-CVOT, estimated based on the following covariates:

- age
- sex
- baseline BMI
- history of coronary revascularization
- history of heart failure
- history of MI
- history of stroke
- T2D duration
- baseline HbA1c
- baseline SBP
- baseline eGFR
- logarithm of baseline UACR
- baseline non-HDL
- smoking habit (current or non-current).

## Results

Propensity score-based weighting analysis estimated that compared with a putative placebo, tirzepatide reduced the risk of

- MACE-3 by 28% (HR = 0.72; 95% CI: 0.55, 0.94),
- all-cause mortality by 39% (HR = 0.61; 95% CI: 0.45, 0.82), and
- CV death or heart failure events by 30% (HR = 0.70; 95% CI: 0.51, 0.96).

The estimated number needed to treat based on indirect comparison for tirzepatide versus placebo was 24 over a 4-year period and 21 over a 5-year period for MACE-3.

The following table presents adjusted and unadjusted indirect treatment effect estimates of tirzepatide versus placebo using data in \"Indirect Treatment Comparison Target Population\" that included all mITT participants from SURPASS-CVOT, and ITT participants from REWIND who would have met inclusion and exclusion criteria for SURPASS-CVOT.
