## 4.4.3. Discussion on clinical efficacy

## Design and conduct of clinical studies

Initially, with this type II variation the following indication (section 4.1 SmPC) was proposed to be added:

Mounjaro is indicated to reduce the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction, or stroke) in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus and established cardiovascular disease.

This new claim was based on the cardiovascular outcome study SURPASS-CVOT. In addition, sections 4.4, 4.8 and 5.1 of the SmPC were updated. The Package Leaflet was also updated accordingly.

## Study design SURPASS-CVOT

SURPASS-CVOT was a phase 3, event-driven, multicenter, randomised, double-blind, active comparator, parallel-group study. The study assessed the efficacy of once weekly tirzepatide maximal tolerated dose on MACE-3 incidence, compared with once weekly dulaglutide 1.5 mg, when added to standard of care in patients with T2D with established CVD. Study participants were randomly assigned 1:1 to tirzepatide or dulaglutide and were stratified by country of enrolment and baseline SGLT-2i use status.

CV outcomes were assessed as they occurred, and other measures were collected every 4 weeks for the first 6 months and then followed approximately every 3 months thereafter.

In line with recent recommendations by the American Diabetes Association (ADA 2017) and European Association for the Study of Diabetes working groups, GLP-1 RAs should be considered for treatment of patients with T2DM and atherosclerotic CVD due to their proven cardiovascular benefits. From an ethical point of view, it might therefore not feasible to withhold GLP-1 RA treatment from patients during a long-term cardiovascular outcome study. Dulaglutide has been used at a dose of 1.5 mg in the REWIND study and showed cardiovascular benefit in terms of MACE-3 reduction versus placebo. Hence, dulaglutide is considered a suitable active comparator. Not on par dosing of TZP and dulaglutide is acceptable, primarily due to the use of dulaglutide 1.5 mg in the cardiovascular outcome study REWIND.

Overall, the study design of SURPASS-CVOT (active control, up-titration period, blinding, duration) is acceptable.

Study population SURPASS-CVOT was a global study that enrolled 13,299 participants with T2D and established CVD from 30 countries. Key inclusion criteria were established CVD disease (coronary artery disease, cerebrovascular disease, peripheral arterial disease), HbA1c between 7% and 10.5%, and a BMI of at least 25 kg/m². The in-and exclusion criteria are considered adequate to identify a T2D population at higher risk for cardiovascular events.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Baseline characteristics

More men than women were randomised (71.0% were male). The study population is sufficiently representative for the European target population (81.4% White, 46.2% from Europe). Overall, the mean age was 64.1 years and the mean BMI was 32.6 kg/m 2 . The mean duration of T2D was 14.7 years, the mean eGFR was 76.5 mL/min per 1.73 m 2 , and the mean percentage of HbA1c was 8.4%. CV disease at baseline: 57.2% patients with coronary revascularization, 47.2% patients with MI, 20.3% patients with HF, and 19.2% patients with stroke. The percentage of participants with a high or very-high risk CKD as defined by the KDIGO guideline was 22.2%. Overall, demographic and baseline characteristics were comparable across the treatment groups.

## Objectives and end points

The primary MACE-3 endpoint and the key secondary endpoints are adequate for investigation of cardiovascular outcome. Fatal and non-fatal endpoint events were centrally adjudicated in a blinded manner. The (key) secondary endpoints investigated components of the cardiovascular composite, a MACE-4 endpoint including revascularisation, a time to event analysis for hospitalisation for heart failure/urgent heart failure visit, hospitalisation for unstable angina as well as cardiometabolic endpoints (HbA1c, blood lipids, body weight, blood pressure). In addition, endpoints exploring the evolution of renal function were defined as secondary endpoints and evaluated in the mITT population as well as in a subset of participants with high- or very-high-risk CKD (KDIGO criteria, 2025). There is a very long list of secondary and explorative endpoints (26 endpoints). Results with respect to these endpoints should be interpreted with caution.

## Statistical methods

Generally, efficacy analyses were based on the modified intention-to-treat (mITT) dataset, which includes data from all randomised participants collected during the follow-up period, regardless of study drug status. Participants who were randomised in error were excluded from the mITT dataset, which is considered justified, as the decision was made before unblinding and the same criteria were applied to both treatment arms.

Although no estimand was explicitly defined, the primary analysis of the primary endpoint based on the mITT dataset is considered to align with the treatment policy strategy for handling intercurrent events related to study drug status, such as treatment interruptions or discontinuations. This approach reflects real-world clinical practice and is regarded relevant for clinical decision-making. In the context of the non-inferiority test, the frequency and distribution of intercurrent events across the two treatment arms are critical for the proper interpretation of the analysis results.

Sensitivity analyses, including multiple imputation of missing follow-up time, tipping point analyses, and an 'on-treatment analysis' were planned to further evaluate the robustness of the conclusions of the primary analysis. These approaches are supported.

For the determination of the non-inferiority (NI) margin for the hazard ratio (HR) of tirzepatide versus dulaglutide regarding the primary endpoint, a Bayesian hierarchical meta-analysis was performed using the secondary prevention cohort from the REWIND CVOT and secondary prevention cohorts from five other GLP-1 RA CVOTs to estimate the effect of dulaglutide versus placebo. An NI margin of 1.05 for comparing tirzepatide and dulaglutide is chosen, which represents the inverse of the 97.5th percentile of the posterior distribution of HR (dulaglutide versus placebo) and ensures preservation of at least 50% of the dulaglutide efficacy if the observed HR (tirzepatide versus dulaglutide) &lt;0.95. The chosen NI margin is acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A graphical approach was pre-specified to control the type I error for the analysis of the primary and key secondary endpoints, taking into account one interim analysis for efficacy. This approach is considered acceptable.

A SAP prespecified indirect comparison of tirzepatide versus placebo was conducted for the primary and CV-related key secondary endpoints utilizing individual participant data from SURPASS-CVOT and REWIND. The analysis included data from REWIND participants who would have been eligible to enrol in SURPASS-CVOT, thus representing a comparable cohort with SURPASS-CVOT, and all SURPASSCVOT participants, and estimated the effect of tirzepatide to reduce the CV risk compared with an indirect placebo. In addition, an indirect comparison sensitivity analysis was conducted utilizing all REWIND participants. For both analyses, the indirect treatment effect was derived by multiplying the HR for SURPASS-CVOT comparing tirzepatide versus dulaglutide by the HR for REWIND comparing dulaglutide versus placebo. HRs were adjusted based on stabilized inverse-probability weight calculated based on the estimated propensity score. Due to the potential bias inherent in the indirect comparison, the results can only be considered supportive, not definitive. Hence, inclusion of results of this indirect comparison in the SmPC (section 5.1) is not warranted.

## Conduct

The study appeared to be well conducted. The high rate of patients who completed the study is considered reassuring (99.1% TZP, 98.9% dulaglutide). Nearly 80% of participants in both treatment groups completed the study on treatment.

## Concomitant medications

At baseline, the use of antihyperglycaemic, antihypertensive, and lipid-lowering medications was well balanced between the two treatment groups. The use of antiplatelets and anticoagulants at baseline was also comparable between the two treatment groups.

Post-baseline, a decrease in the percentage of participants using SUs and insulins was observed in the tirzepatide treatment group. At Month 36, the tirzepatide group had a significantly lower percentage of participants using SUs and insulins compared with the dulaglutide group (SUs: tirzepatide group: 15.0%; dulaglutide group: 21.4%, and Insulins: tirzepatide group: 39.2%; dulaglutide group: 47.8%).

During the course of the study, an increase in the percentage of participants using SGLT 2i was observed in the dulaglutide treatment group. At Month 36, the tirzepatide group had a significantly lower percentage of participants using SGLT-2i (33.8%) compared with the dulaglutide group (41.1%). This may have impacted results on renal endpoints (see OCs, RSI).

Post-baseline differences in the use of SUs, insulin, and SGLT2i (with higher use in the dulaglutide group) were expectable due to the distinct antihyperglycaemic potency of TZP which was partly caused by using the lowest approved dose of dulaglutide and the maximally tolerated dose for TZP.

## Exposure

No significant differences were observed between the treatment groups in follow-up time in study. The median follow-up time in study was 210.7 weeks for tirzepatide and 210.0 weeks for dulaglutide. The percentage of participants with follow-up time in study of 36 months or greater was similar between the treatment groups (tirzepatide: 90.5%; dulaglutide: 89.5%). No significant differences were observed between the treatment groups in treatment duration. The median duration of study ontreatment was 203.1 weeks for tirzepatide and 204.1 weeks for dulaglutide.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Procedures described in the protocol for up- and downtitration of TZP as well as sham dose-deescalation in the dulaglutide treated patients are acceptable.

In this study all participants underwent titration with the goal of reaching the TZP 15 g dose, which was achieved by 72.7% of participants (17.6% were treated with 5 mg TZP, 9.2% were treated with 10 mg TZP). Hence, the vast majority of patients were treated with the maximum dose of 15 mg TZP (according to section 4.2 of the SmPC: \"The recommended maintenance doses are 5mg, 10 mg and 15 mg. The maximum dose is 15 mg\").

Hence, different dose levels have been used for tirzepatide and dulaglutide; the latter was applied as the lowest approved 1.5 mg dose.

Nevertheless, this dosing approach appears acceptable, as up-titration of tirzepatide to maximally tolerated doses was likewise applied in other phase 3 studies and is in line with the SmPC. In addition, dulaglutide was applied at the dose used in REWIND CVOT. Use of higher dulaglutide doses would have rendered transferability of results as well as a definition of a non-inferiority margin difficult.

## Efficacy data and additional analyses

## Primary endpoint MACE-3

The primary composite outcome occurred in 801 (12.2%) participants (3.25 events per 100 PY of follow-up) in the tirzepatide group and 862 (13.1%) participants (3.54 events per 100 PY of follow-up) in the dulaglutide group. Noninferiority of MACE-3 was confirmed for tirzepatide compared to dulaglutide with a HR of 0.92 (95.3% CI: 0.83, 1.01; noninferiority p-value = 0.007). The upper bound of the CI (1.01) was less than the prespecified noninferiority margin of 1.05. Superiority of tirzepatide compared to dulaglutide was not achieved (p-value = 0.086).

The effect was consistent across the three components of MACE; the HRs (95% CI) were 0.89 (0.77, 1.02) for death from CV or undetermined causes, 0.86 (0.74, 1.00) for MI, and 0.91 (0.76, 1.09) for stroke. Total events (first and recurrent combined) were less frequent with TZP compared to dulaglutide, with similar HRs for MACE and its individual components as in the primary time to first event analysis.

The treatment effect of tirzepatide was consistent with the primary outcome across most of the subgroups, including sex, age, HbA1c, and SGLT-2i use at baseline. The HR exceeded 1 in the subgroups per region of enrolment (North America), and baseline BMI category ≥27kg/m 2 to ≤30kg/m 2 . As regards the latter finding, no trend was observed across BMI categories; in the subgroup with baseline BMI 25-27kg/m2 the HR 0.76 (0.60-0.98) indicated a benefit in the lower range of overweight patients; a benefit was likewise observed in patients with baseline BMI&gt;30kg/m 2 (HR 0.90, CI 0.79-1.01). Hence, the apparent lack of benefit in patients in the subgroup ≥27kg/m 2 to ≤30kg/m 2 (HR 1.11, CI 0.91-1.37) most likely is a chance finding.

Generally, results of subgroup analyses have to be interpreted with caution. This may particularly apply to subgroup analyses in cardiovascular outcome studies due to the low number of outcome events, which may additionally enhance variability. Overall, results in subgroups can be regarded as consistent with the results in the overall study population.

## Key secondary endpoints

Since superiority of tirzepatide versus dulaglutide was not established, formal statistical comparison between treatment groups of key secondary endpoints was not controlled for family-wise Type 1 error rate and p-values are nominal.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The incidences of all-cause death (HR 0.84, CI 0.75, 0.94), time to CV death (HR 0.89, CI 0.77, 1.02), time to first occurrence of CV death or HFE (HR 0.91, CI 0.81, 1.03), time to first occurrence of MACE4 (HR 0.88, CI 0.81, 0.96) were lower in the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group. The difference in deaths was most pronounced for non-cardiovascular deaths; infections caused 1.2% of non-cardiovascular deaths in the tirzepatide group and 2.1% of non-cardiovascular deaths in the dulaglutide group. According to published literature, the magnitude of weight reduction and glycaemic improvement achieved with TZP can be sufficient for reducing infections in obese and diabetic subjects by the extent observed in SURPASS-CVOT. The HR for CV death or HFE was not significantly different between the two treatment groups.

The change in eGFR from baseline to week 36 was primarily explored in a subgroup of the SURPASSCVOT population. The criteria used to identify this subgroup with high risk for CKD progression at baseline are acceptable; they are in line with KDIGO recommendations (de Boer et al., Kidney International 2022; 102, 974-989). Results were also provided for the entire ITT population, which consisted of patients with and without CKD.

Dulaglutide can be considered a suitable active control for investigating TZP`s renoprotective potential. In a REWIND post-hoc analysis (Botros et al., 2023), dulaglutide showed a small effect on slowing the eGFR decline compared to placebo (mean decline in eGFR dulaglutide vs placebo in the overall population -1.37 vs. -1.56 ml/min/1.73 m 2 /year; mean decline in eGFR dulaglutide vs placebo in the population with baseline UACR&gt;30 mg/g -1.73 vs -2.05 ml/min/1.73 m 2 /year). In the same study, results for renal outcome events were in favour of dulaglutide resulting in a risk reduction of 25% for the composite renal outcome endpoint. Further, results from the AWARD-7 study showed renally beneficial effects with dulaglutide as compared to insulin glargine in a population of moderately or severely impaired renal subjects; especially, there was a notable difference in effect size with respect to proteinuria reduction (-43% with dulaglutide; -14.3% with placebo). Taken together, no detrimental action of dulaglutide on the kidney became evident. It can be regarded to exhibit renoprotection at least on a placebo level.

The decline in eGFR in the subset of patients at risk of CKD progression was nominally significantly reduced in the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group at Month 36 (difference between treatment groups 3.17 mL/min/1.73 m 2 , 95% CI: 2.09, 4.26; use of the CKD-EPI Creatinine-Cystatin Equation 2021). In the entire mITT population, the decline in eGFR was also reduced in the TZP group compared with the dulaglutide group, but to a lesser extent.

The hold of eGFR decline was more pronounced in the subgroup of patients with SGLT2i use at baseline, both in the TZP and in the dulaglutide group (mean change from baseline at month 36; group with SGLT2i at baseline -5.40 TZP, -6.26 dulaglutide; group without SGLT2i use at baseline -7.99 TZP, -8.87 dulaglutide).

To further explore the impact of concomitant SGLT2i use on eGFR results, the MAH provided results for a third category of patients with no SGLT2i use at all (not at baseline and not during the course of the study) to explore TZP`s distinct renoprotective action. Results showed a more pronounced halt of eGFR decline in patients on TZP compared to dulaglutide: eGFR changed by -5.44% from baseline to Month 36 in the TZP group and by -8.85% in the dulaglutide group. The observed difference between treatment arms (3.41 ml/min/1.73 m 2 ) was well in line with the treatment differences in the subgroups with SGLT2i at baseline/without SGLT2i at baseline who commenced SGLT2i therapy during the study. It is agreed with the Applicant that TZP reduced the decline in eGFR to a greater extent as compared with dulaglutide independent of SGLT-2i use.

The slope analysis showed that the annual rate of decline in eGFR for participants with high risk of CKD progression was nominally significantly slower for participants in the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group (0.93 mL/min/1.73m 2 per year, 95% CI: 0.65, 1.22, p-value &lt;0.001). The sustained separation of eGFR trajectories between treatment arms may indicate that the long-term renal benefit in the TZP arm is not attributable solely to the early acute change; the underlying mechanism of the acute change in eGFR remained unclear. Weight loss and improved glycaemic control may have played a role.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, these data support superior nephroprotection of TZP compared to dulaglutide. This benefit is adequately reflected in 5.1 of the SmPC.

## Selected secondary and exploratory endpoints

HbA1c: the reduction in HbA1c from baseline to Month 36 was nominally significantly greater in the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group (-1.66% with TZP versus -0.88% with dulaglutide). The relatively higher TZP dose (most of the patients were up-titrated to 15 mg, dulaglutide was used at its lowest approved dose of 1.5 mg) contributed to the difference in effect size. However, results from study GBGL, the pivotal study submitted in the Trulicity line extension (adding dulaglutide doses of 3 mg and 4.5 mg), showed a rather flat dose response (dulaglutide 1.5 mg: - 1.53%, dulaglutide 3 mg: 1.71 %, dulaglutide 4.5 mg 1.87%), which implies that potential use of the highest dulaglutide dose may not have led to markedly different results.

The relative effect size of dulaglutide in REWIND on reduction in HbA1c was 0.61% (dulaglutide versus placebo) indicating that TZP was compared to an antihyperglycaemic efficacious comparator.

Overall, TZP led to a clinically relevant additional reduction in HbA1c compared to dulaglutide.

Body weight and Waist circumference: body weight was nominally significantly reduced with tirzepatide (mostly at a dose of 15 mg) compared to dulaglutide 1.5 mg (-11.6% change in body weight with TZP; -4.8% change in body weight with dulaglutide 1.5 mg). Use of not on-par doses of TZP and dulaglutide might have contributed to the difference in effect size; however, as the dose-response curve with respect to body weight reduction across dulaglutide doses of 1.5 mg, 3 mg, and 4.5 mg was rather flat (study GBGL results, % change from BL in body weight to week 36: dulaglutide 1.5 mg: -3.1 kg, for dulaglutide 3.0 mg: -4.0 kg, and for dulaglutide 4.5 mg: -4.7 kg) even in the event the highest dulaglutide 4.5 mg dose had been used this would unlikely have altered conclusions.

Reassuringly, body weight reduction went along with a greater and nominally significant reduction in waist circumference with TZP compared to dulaglutide (-9.0cm with TZP; -3.5cm with dulaglutide) which is suggestive of a greater loss of (abdominal) fat mass. In the absence of a DEXA scan reduction in waist circumference is indicative of the desirable loss of fat mass (opposed to loss of muscle mass).

Overall, TZP led to a clinically relevant reduction in body weight and waist circumference compared to dulaglutide.

## Other endpoints

There were slight improvements in systolic blood pressure and lipid profile. The difference between treatment groups on reduction of SBP (-2.1 mmHg) is considered clinically relevant. Except for LDL cholesterol, TZP led to numerically greater improvements in lipid parameters compared to dulaglutide.

PROs were investigated by three validated questionnaires (Ability to Perform Physical Activities of Daily Living, APPADL; Impact of Weight on Self Perception, IW-SP; EQ-5D-5L ,EuroQoL Group, 2019). Results showed very small numerical improvements which mostly were below the minimally clinically relevant differences. It can be concluded that the benefit with respect to weight loss and improvement in glycaemic control did not translate into a marked improvement of the patient`s perceived wellbeing.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Ancillary analysis

A SAP prespecified indirect comparison of tirzepatide versus placebo was conducted for the primary and CV-related key secondary endpoints utilizing individual participant data from SURPASS-CVOT and REWIND. The analysis included data from REWIND participants who would have been eligible to enrol in SURPASS-CVOT, and all SURPASS-CVOT participants, and estimated the effect of tirzepatide to reduce the CV risk compared with an indirect placebo. In addition, an indirect comparison sensitivity analysis was conducted utilizing all REWIND participants.

For both analyses, the indirect treatment effect was derived by multiplying the HR for SURPASS-CVOT comparing tirzepatide versus dulaglutide by the HR for REWIND comparing dulaglutide versus placebo. HRs were adjusted based on stabilized inverse-probability weight calculated based on the estimated propensity score. Missing covariates were handled with multiple imputation. Number needed to treat for MACE-3 was indirectly estimated at the median follow-up of 4 years in SURPASS-CVOT, based on the same manner as the estimation of indirect treatment effect expressed as HR.

Propensity score-based weighting analysis estimated that compared with a putative placebo, tirzepatide reduced the risk of

- MACE-3 by 28% (HR = 0.72; 95% CI: 0.55, 0.94),
- all-cause mortality by 39% (HR = 0.61; 95% CI: 0.45, 0.82), and
- CV death or heart failure events by 30% (HR = 0.70; 95% CI: 0.51, 0.96).

The estimated number needed to treat based on indirect comparison for tirzepatide versus placebo was 24 over a 4-year period and 21 over a 5-year period for MACE-3. A sensitivity analysis using the entire REWIND population was performed and led to similar results.

As outlined above, these results are considered supportive of the primary findings from the SURPASSCVOT. No inclusion of these results in section in 5.1 of the SmPC is warranted (see annotated PI).

Based on the sensitivity analysis, imputation of missing follow-up time for 136 participants did not affect the non-inferiority conclusion in the primary analysis. In addition, the tipping point sensitivity analysis showed that the primary endpoint event rate in patients who did not complete the trials had been 9.7 times higher than the estimated event rate to reverse the non-inferiority conclusion. An almost 10-fold increase is clinically implausible. Hence, based on these analyses imputation of missing follow-up time had very unlikely altered the non-inferiority conclusion.

Important protocol deviations occurred at comparable frequencies in both treatment groups and are considered without relevant impact on the integrity of the analyses.

Patients who were found to have been randomised in error were excluded from efficacy analyses. These participants experienced 5 (tirzepatide, 3; dulaglutide, 2) CEC-confirmed primary endpoints. The low number and equal distribution of these events makes it unlikely that the exclusion of these data had an impact on study results.

## 4.4.4. Conclusions on the clinical efficacy

SURPASS-CVOT demonstrated that TZP once weekly is non-inferior in terms of lowering the incidence of MACE-3 compared to dulaglutide, both added to standard of care in subjects with established CV disease and T2DM. Priorly, dulaglutide had shown to lower the incidence of MACE-3 compared to placebo in a CVOT (REWIND study). Based on this study, an update of the SmPC in line with the current CHMP policy on the wording of therapeutic indications was granted by the CHMP. In addition to achieving non-inferiority as regards MACE-3, a reduction was shown for all-cause mortality, monocomponents of the primary composite endpoint, HbA1c, body weight and systolic blood pressure. In a subset of renally impaired patients TZP slowed progression of renal disease to a greater extent than dulaglutide (less loss of eGFR from baseline, flatter curve of the eGFR slope).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Therapeutic indication

The indication initially proposed by the Applicant

Mounjaro is indicated to reduce the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction, or stroke) in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus and established cardiovascular disease.

was found not acceptable. This is due to the CHMP policy which views cardiovascular outcome events (myocardial infarction, stroke, cardiovascular death) as important health outcomes associated with T2DM. As such, a crucial aim of antihyperglycaemic therapy is to prevent these events. In the submitted study these adverse outcomes were non-inferior with TZP compared to dulaglutide in T2DM patients with higher cardiovascular risk. Hence, the beneficial effect of TZP is already sufficiently covered by the current T2DM indication. This was accepted by the Applicant, and the a.m. indication is no longer claimed. Section 4.1 of the SmPC has been revised accordingly (see below, new text in bold).

## \"Type 2 diabetes mellitus

Mounjaro is indicated for the treatment of adults, adolescents and children aged 10 years and above with insufficiently controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus as an adjunct to diet and exercise

- as monotherapy when metformin is considered inappropriate due to intolerance or contraindications
- in addition to other medicinal products for the treatment of diabetes.

For study results with respect to combinations, effects on glycaemic control, the populations studied, and cardiovascular events see sections 4.4, 4.5 and 5.1.

## 4.5. Clinical safety

## Introduction

Safety analyses are presented by the applicant for the safety dataset, which included all randomised participants who took at least 1 dose of study drug. All events that occurred between the date of first dose of study drug to the date of the participant's end of follow-up, regardless of adherence to study drug, were included.

Special attention was drawn by the applicant to the participants who experienced any of the following:

- death
- gallbladder disease
- severe or serious gallbladder and biliary tract disorders
- liver injury
- potential hepatocellular drug-induced liver injury
- severe or serious renal events
- severe or serious pancreatic malignancy
- severe or serious C-cell hyperplasia and thyroid malignancy

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- other serious malignancies
- serious suicidal attempts and ideation
- serious ophthalmologic cases (including non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy [NAION])
- severe or serious diabetic retinopathy complications
- serious malnutrition
- serious aspiration under general anaesthesia or deep sedation
- pregnancy (including partner pregnancy)
- serious diabetic ketoacidosis
- severe hypoglycaemia
- serious syncope
- severe or serious atrial fibrillation and atrial flutter
- severe or serious arrhythmias and cardiac conduction disorders (excluding atrial fibrillation and atrial flutter)
- severe or serious adverse events in GI SOC
- severe or serious adjudication-confirmed pancreatitis
- severe or serious hypersensitivity reaction
- severe or serious amputation
- severe or serious peripheral revascularization
- other SAEs.

## Patient exposure

In this study, follow-up time was defined as time between date of randomisation to the date of end-offollow-up plus 1 day. Duration of exposure in days was defined as time in days from date of first dose of study drug to date of last dose of study drug plus 7 days, excluding temporary interruptions in days.

At Month 36, 4922 [74.7%] participants in the tirzepatide group and 4983 [75.7%] participants in the dulaglutide group remained on study drug (Table GPGN.8.21). Of those receiving tirzepatide,

- 867 (17.6%) tirzepatide-treated participants were receiving 5 mg
- 451 (9.2%) tirzepatide-treated participants were receiving 10 mg
- 3580 (72.7%) tirzepatide-treated participants were receiving 15 mg

The median follow-up time in study was 210.7 weeks for tirzepatide and 210.0 weeks for dulaglutide.

The percentage of participants with follow-up time in study of 36 months or greater was similar between the treatment groups (tirzepatide: 90.5%; dulaglutide: 89.5%). For details see table below.

## AEs by SOC and PT

Regarding the types of AEs, it became obvious that most of them were related to the gastrointestinal (GI) tract, i.e. SOC of gastrointestinal disorders. This is expected for GLP-1 RAs. Notably, the frequency of GI effects was higher in the TZP than in the dulaglutide group, 60.8% vs. 54.3%, see table below. A small numerical difference between TZP and dulaglutide also observed for the SOC \"Metabolism and nutrition disorders\" (30.5% vs. 26.0%). This may be related to the PT \"Weight decreased\", see PT table further below.

The following table lists the AEs by PT. Again, PTs related to GI disorders are prominent and generally more frequent with TZP compared to dulaglutide. Besides GI effects, numerical differences were observed in respect to hypotension (more frequent with TZP) and hypertension (less frequent with TZP compared to dula). Effects of GLP1-RAs on heart rate and blood pressure are known. A marked difference was also observed for the PT \"Weight decreased\". This is considered related to the desired effect of GLP-1 RAs. Furthermore, asthenia and dizziness were more frequent with TZP than with dulaglutide. An increased frequency of these types of AEs with GLP-1 RA treatment was also observed in in previous studies.

The differences between TZP and dulaglutide in respect to GLP-1-related effects can be explained by the fact that TZP was given as maximally tolerated dose whereas the dose of dulaglutide was rather low.

The reason for the increased infection rate with dulaglutide compared to TZP in SURPASS-CVOT is not clear. For further insight, infection events observed in a CVOT study (REWIND) with dulaglutide 1.5 mg compared to placebo will be described in the following.

In REWIND, death due to infection was numerically less frequent with dulaglutide than with plc (0.9% dulaglutide vs. 1.2% plc), see table below.

## AEs leading to discontinuation

Higher proportions of participants in the tirzepatide group permanently discontinued study drug due to AEs in the following SOCs:

- Gastrointestinal disorders (tirzepatide: 438 [6.6%]; dulaglutide: 327 [4.9%])
- o At the PT level under the Gastrointestinal disorders SOC, permanent study drug discontinuation due to the TEAE of Nausea was higher in the tirzepatide group.
- Metabolism and nutrition disorders (tirzepatide: 76 [1.1%]; dulaglutide: 38 [0.6%])

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- o At the PT level under the Investigations SOC, permanent study drug discontinuation due to the TEAE of Decreased appetite was higher in the tirzepatide group.
- Investigations (tirzepatide: 76 [1.1%]; dulaglutide: 38 [0.6%])
- o At the PT level under the Investigations SOC, permanent study drug discontinuation due to the TEAE of Weight decreased was higher in the tirzepatide group.

The KM plot below clearly shows a higher discontinuation rate with TZP. The curves for TZP and dulaglutide, respectively, most pronouncedly diverted between Week 26 and Week 52.

An overview of serious and severe GI events is provided in the following.

## Serious GI events

- Tirzepatide: 279 (4.2%) participants experienced at least 1 serious GI event.
- Dulaglutide: 248 (3.7%) participants experienced at least 1 serious GI event.

## Severe GI events

- Tirzepatide: 353 (5.3%) participants experienced at least 1 severe GI event.
- Dulaglutide: 274 (4.1%) participants experienced at least 1severe GI event.

The most common serious or severe TEAEs in tirzepatide-treated participants in the GI SOC were:

- Diarrhoea (tirzepatide, 84 [1.3%]; dulaglutide, 56 [0.8%])
- Nausea (tirzepatide, 77 [1.2%]; dulaglutide, 56 [0.8%]), and
- Vomiting (tirzepatide, 64 [1.0%]; dulaglutide, 43 [0.6%]).

Severity and prevalence are graphically shown in the figure below. The GI events were mild in most cases; only a small fraction was severe. The events mainly occurred shortly after start of treatment. Thereafter, the prevalence of GI events stabilised at a certain level, around 2% to 3%, see figure below. It took noticeably longer with TZP than with dulaglutide until the prevalence of GI events had reached the stable level, around 78 weeks in case of TZP compared to around 52 weeks with dula.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Kidney

AEs of kidney injury

Overall, similar percentages of participants in the tirzepatide group and dulaglutide group experienced at least 1 treatment-emergent renal disorder. The most frequently reported PTs in both treatment groups were

- Acute kidney injury (tirzepatide, 226 [3.4]; dulaglutide, 178 [2.7]) and
- Chronic kidney disease (tirzepatide, 147 [2.2]; dulaglutide, 170 [2.6]).

## eGFR

The following figure shows the course of eGFR over time (study report pp4282-4297). In the TZP group, eGFR essentially remained constant in the first 52 weeks of treatment. Thereafter, there was as steady decline until Week 208. In the dulaglutide group, there was eGFR decline essentially from the beginning of treatment. The decline is most likely related to the underlying disease (diabetes mell.).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 19 - Plot of estimated mean eGFR (CKD-EPI Creatinine Equation 2021) by treatment and visit from baseline to end of follow-up for the mITT dataset

<!-- image -->

The difference in eGFR decline over time between TZP and dulaglutide is further addressed in the following two figures. It turned out that particularly in subjects with baseline eGFR &lt;60 mL/min/1.73 m2 and in subjects with SGLT2i use the difference between TZP and dulaglutide was most pronounced. In these subjects, TZP caused a marked increase in eGFR immediately after start of treatment. The reason for this effect is not known.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Q

Figure 20 - Plot of eGFR (CKD-EPI Creatinine Equation 2021) by non-missing baseline eGFR (&lt;60 and ≥60 mL/min/1.73 m2) by treatment and visit from baseline to end of follow-up for the mITT dataset

<!-- image -->

Figure 21 - Plot of eGFR (CKD-EPI Creatinine Equation 2021) by SGLT-2i use at baseline by treatment and visit from baseline to end of follow-up for the mITT dataset

<!-- image -->

The apparent initial eGFR increase with TZP as described above may be an artifact due to GFR estimation by serum creatinine. When GFR is estimated using serum cystatin C, see figure below, no initial eGFR increase becomes obvious with TZP. The curves of TZP and dulaglutide are similar; a slightly slower eGFR decline was observed with TZP compared to dulaglutide. This effect was more pronounced in subjects with high- or very-high-risk CKD as defined by KDIGO.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 22 - Plot of estimated mean eGFR (CKD-EPI Cystatin C Equation 2012) by treatment and visit from baseline to end of follow-up for the mITT dataset

<!-- image -->

The applicant did not provide eGFR values over time that were calculated with the 2021 creatininecystatin C equation, i.e. with the equation using both parameters, creatinine and cystatin C. Only tables showing shifts of eGFR category from baseline to study end was provided for eGFR values calculated with this equation. According to these tables, slightly more subjects treated with TZP shifted to a higher category (i.e. better eGFR) compared to dulaglutide. Vice versa, slightly less subjects shifted to a lower category with TZP than with dulaglutide. Since the applicant regarded eGFR improvement as an efficacy endpoint, eGFR changes are also covered in the efficacy part.

Urinary Albumin/Creatinine Ratio (UACR)

The effect of dulaglutide and TZP on UACR was provided by the applicant as a shift table, see below. A higher percentage of subjects treated with TZP, compared to dulaglutide, shifted to a lower UACR during the study (10.1% vs. 5.8%). Accordingly, a lower percentage shifted to a higher group with TZP compared to dulaglutide.

| Shifted to lower UACR category, n (%)  | 671 (10.1)             | 383 (5.8)              |

It is known that GLP-1 RAs increase pancreas markers in serum (lipase and amylase) without inducing pancreatitis. Serum lipase over time is shown in the figure below. ## Thyroid

## Neoplasms

Animal studies of various GLP-1 RAs have shown hyperplasia, adenomas and carcinomas of the thyroid C-cells in rodents. These changes were accompanied by marked increases in serum calcitonin. In humans, most marketed GLP-1 RAs do not induce an increase in serum calcitonin whereas TZP caused a slight increase (see respective paragraph further below). The relevance is unknown.

As shown in the following table, nine cases of papillary and two cases of medullary thyroid cancer were observed in the TZP group of SURPASS-CVOT. In the dulaglutide group, a total of two thyroid malignancies was observed, one papillary and one follicular cancer. The reason for the relatively high number of papillary tumours in the TZP group is unknown. Papillary thyroid cancer is unrelated to calcitonin. Regarding medullary cancer of the C-cells, this tumour mainly occurs in subjects with multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2, and these subjects were not included in SURPASS-CVOT. In subjects not carrying a gene mutation in the RET proto-oncogen, the risk for medullary thyroid cancer is low.

The applicant collected incidence data of thyroid cancer from literature. According to the sources, papillary carcinoma is most frequent, followed by follicular and medullary carcinoma. The applicant cited publications stating the incidence of thyroid cancer being around 11 per 100000 patient years (py) in Europe and around 13.5 cases per 100000 py in the US. The US figures for papillary, follicular and medullary cancer were 11.9, 0.8 and 0.2 per 100000 py, respectively, according to the applicant's sources (p53 of SCS). In the SURPASS-CVOT trial, exposure was 25637 py in the TZP group. Hence, the observed event numbers are higher than expected, for papillary as well as for medullary tumours. However, the absolute numbers are low so that no reliable conclusions can be drawn.

| Subjects with >= 1 TEAE of C-Cell Hyperplasia and Thyroid | 11 (0.2)           | 2 (0.0)               | 13 (0.1)          | 0.14(0.03, 0.24)              | 0.022       |
| Malignancies Papillary thyroid cancer                     | 9 (0.1)            | 1 (0.0)               | 10 (0.1)          | 0.12(0.03, 0.21)              | 0.021       |
| Medullary thyroid cancer                                  | 2 (0.0)            | 0                     | 2 (0.0)           | 0.03(-0.01, 0.07)             | 0.500       |

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

| Follicular thyroid cancer   | 0   | 1 (0.0)   | 1 (0.0)   | -0.02(-0.04,   | 0.01)   | >.999   |
|-----------------------------|-----|-----------|-----------|----------------|---------|---------|

The narratives of the two cases of medullary thyroid cancer (MTC) are given in the following:

## Case 1:

Participant, 50-59 years, White, Male,

AE of \"serum calcitonin elevation\" reported, 90.6 ng/L (mild), leading to drug interruption.

US with suspicious thyroid nodule.

Total thyroidectomy; pathology: MTC 5×5×4 mm, no lymphovascular/perineural invasion RET negative (blood and tumour)

The AE was considered serious, the outcome was listed as \"recovered\".

Participant, 60-69 years, White, Male,

AE of \"elevated calcitonin\" reported, 36.2 ng/L (mild).

micronodule in left lobe.

FNA with very high calcitonin Total thyroidectomy + central compartment dissection. Pathology: MTC 8×6 mm, no capsular/extrathyroidal invasion RET negative (blood); RET positive (tumour, pathogenic variant)

The AE was considered serious, the outcome was listed as \"recovered\".

## Hypoglycaemia

TZP alone hardly caused severe hypoglycaemia as shown in the table below (in around 0.2% of subjects). As expected, the highest hypoglycaemia rate was observed in subjects on hypoglycaemic background therapy. In subjects receiving insulin and SU, TZP had a stronger hypoglycaemic effect than dulaglutide (2.9% vs. 1.3% of subjects; event rate of 0.009 vs. 0.003, TZP vs. dula).

## Diabetic ketoacidosis

Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) was not pre-defined as an AESI, but GLP-1 RA may pose a risk for DKA because they reduce food intake. DKA was reported as an AE; it was reported in 18 (0.3%) subjects with TZP and in 13 (0.2%) subjects receiving dulaglutide. Hence, the incidence was low and gives no cause for concern.

## Malignancies

Overall, slightly more malignancies were observed in the TZP than in the dulaglutide group, 4.6% vs. 4.1%. The most frequent tumours were carcinomas of the prostate and colon. Prostate and endometrium cancer were numerically more frequent with TZP than with dulaglutide.

## Cardiac Arrhythmias

The TEAEs of arrhythmias were identified using the MedDRA PTs contained within the following SMQs:

- Arrhythmia-related investigations, signs, and symptoms (broad and narrow terms)
- Supraventricular tachyarrhythmia (narrow terms)
- Tachyarrhythmia terms, non-specific (narrow terms), and
- Ventricular tachyarrhythmia (narrow terms).

The TEAEs of cardiac conduction disorders were identified using the PTs contained within

- Conduction defects SMQ (narrow terms), and
- Cardiac conduction disorders HLT.

The applicant's analyses did not reveal any consistent or meaningful differences between TZP and dulaglutide.

## Diabetic retinopathy

The results from the safety data set of the main study, i.e. not only subjects from the retinopathy substudy, are presented in the following.

A composite diabetic retinopathy (DR) endpoint was defined in the main study. It consisted of

- retinal photocoagulation
- vitreous haemorrhage
- vitrectomy
- treatment with intravitreal agents, or
- onset of diabetes-related significant visual loss.

The time to first evet for this composite endpoint is shown as KM plot below. According to this figure, DR progression was slower in the TZP than in the dulaglutide group (HR=0.90). However, the difference was not statistically significant.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 26 - Kaplan-Meier plot of time to first occurrence of composite diabetic retinopathy endpoint for the safety dataset

<!-- image -->

The outcome of the composite endpoint was mainly driven by the component \"first occurrence of retinal photocoagulation\". This component is shown in the KM plot below. In line with the composite endpoint, it took longer for TZP than for dulaglutide until the first photocoagulation treatment (HR=0.82, not statistically significant).

Figure 27 - Kaplan-Meier plot of time to first occurrence of retinal photocoagulation for the safety dataset

<!-- image -->

For the other components of the composite endpoint, the hazard ratios were between 0.41 and 1.06.

Fundoscopy was performed at baseline, after 6 months and after 12 months. The results are displayed in the table below. The number and percentage of patients with proliferative DR was imbalanced at baseline; it was more frequent in the TZP than in the dulaglutide group (41.5% vs. 28.6%). In both treatment groups the prevalence of proliferative DR decreased, but due to the baseline imbalance, it is

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

difficult to delineate whether or not TZP is inferior to comparator dulaglutide in respect to DR progression.

## Substudy Retinopathy

Study Title: The Effect of Tirzepatide versus Dulaglutide on Major Adverse Cardiovascular Events in Participants with Type 2 Diabetes (SURPASS-CVOT) - Diabetic Retinopathy Addendum

## Study Number: I8F-MC-GPGN (7.1)

The SURPASS-CVOT DR addendum aimed to compare the effect of tirzepatide maximum tolerated dose (up to 15 mg once weekly) with dulaglutide (1.5 mg once-weekly) on diabetic retinopathy (DR) progression. Patients with DR or macular oedema (ME) or patients without DR or ME but high-risk for developing DR were included. These patients received additional ophthalmology assessments at baseline and during the addendum maintenance period.

## Number of Study Sites, Participants, and Countries:

The retinopathy addendum was conducted at 106 sites and randomly assigned 920 participants in 14 countries in North America, South America, Europe, and the Asia-Pacific regions.

## Study Period:

First participant first visit for retinopathy addendum: 08 March 2021

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Last participant last visit for retinopathy addendum: 29 May 2025.

## Main study endpoints:

Table 62 - Objectives, Endpoints, and Statistical Methods

| Objectives                                                                                                            | Endpoints                                                                                                                                            |
|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Primary To compare the effect of tirzepatide MTD (up to 15 mg) QW with dulaglutide 1.5 mg QW on DR progression.       | At least 2 grades worsening of DR per person on ETDRS Severity Scale - ETDRS2+ from baseline to 36 months visit                                      |
| Key Secondary To compare the effect of tirzepatide MTD (up to 15 mg) QW with dulaglutide 1.5 mg QW on DR progression. | Time to first onset of ETDRS2+ per person Time to first onset of sustained worsening of visual acuity of at least 3 lines (15 letters) on either eye |

## Primary endpoint analysis, methodology

The primary endpoint was analysed using a logistic regression analysis with treatment, baseline sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 inhibitor use (Yes or No), baseline haemoglobin A1c (HbA1c), and duration of type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2D) as fixed effects, and baseline value of Early Treatment Diabetic Retinopathy Study (ETDRS) scale as a covariate.

The substudy will conclude that tirzepatide is not associated with clinically meaningful increased risk for worsening DR status compared to dulaglutide, if the upper bound of the 95% confidence interval (CI) for odds ratio (tirzepatide versus dulaglutide) for at least 2 grades worsening of DR per person using ETDRS scale from randomisation through 36 months is less than a non-inferiority margin of 1.8fold.

Missing ETDRS scale was imputed, assuming \"missing at random\" with multiple imputation.

The analyses intend to estimate the \"treatment policy strategy\" based parameters of interest, i.e., irrespective of adherence to study drug or introduction of additional treatment for retinopathy.

## Key secondary endpoint analysis

Time-to-event endpoints were based on Cox proportional hazard model stratified by sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 inhibitor use (Yes or No) at baseline, with treatment, baseline HbA1c, and duration of T2D as fixed effects, and baseline value as a covariate. Participants who received DR treatment during the follow-up period were considered to have experienced an event on the date of DR treatment.

## Number of Participants (planned and analysed) in the SURPASS-CVOT DR addendum:

number planned in addendum: 700

number randomised in addendum: 920

number completed addendum on study drug: 586 (63.7%)

number completed addendum: 644 (70%), based on having fundus photographs at Visit 24 or within 6 months thereafter.

## Premature study drug discontinuation

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A total of 334 participants (36.3%) in the addendum discontinued the study drug prematurely, with no overall significant difference between treatment groups. Of these participants, 54 (5.9%) discontinued study drug due to death.

The most common reason for premature discontinuation of study drug was adverse event (tirzepatide, 10.7%; dulaglutide, 7.4%). A total of 12.2% of participants in the tirzepatide group and 15.3% of participants in the dulaglutide group missed the final fundus photograph exam (at Visit 24 or within 6 months thereafter).

## Diagnosis and main criteria for inclusion and exclusion:

Participants who agreed to participate in the SURPASS-CVOT DR addendum, which required periodic fundus photographs, visual acuity testing and dilated fundoscopic exams, must have been eligible for the main study and fulfilled one of the following criteria at baseline:

any evidence of DR or ME in either eye according to the baseline ophthalmologic investigation, or

irrespective of retinal status, the participant was at high risk for developing, recurring, or progressing DR due to the duration of T2D for at least 15 calendar years and HbA1c ≥8% (≥64 mmol/mol) at screening.

## Central grading of retinal fundus photographs

Participants were planned to undergo 7-field or 4-widefield digital colour fundus photography after dilated fundoscopic exam. The fundus photographs were graded according to the Early Treatment Diabetic Retinopathy Study (ETDRS) Diabetic Retinopathy Severity Scale (DRSS) (ETDRS research group, ETDRS Report No10, 1991). The ETDRS DRSS is a tool for recording retinopathy status using a scale sensitive for well-defined changes within and between various severities of non-proliferative diabetic retinopathy and proliferative diabetic retinopathy (PDR).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The ETDRS provides reference-ETDRS fundus photographs and in comparison to them, the participants fundus photographs are categorized in different grades. The focus of interest in the photographs were i.e. microaneurysms, haemorrhages, drusen, hard exudates, soft exudates (cotton-wool patches), microvascular/arteriolar/venous abnormalities, vitreous haemorrhages or other extraretinal abnormalities. Each DRSS Score counts for a different clinical category.

The pictures were evaluated by the central reading center, which was blinded to study drug. The slightly modified and abbreviated ETDRS Severity Scale for individual eyes was used. The ETDRS Severity Scale grades for the individual eyes were combined per person, and these personal scores were the primary subject of the analyses.

DR treatment was defined as treatment with focal laser photocoagulation, pan-retinal laser photocoagulation, or intravitreal injection.

The ETDRS grading was performed centrally and adjudicated based on standard retinal fundus photographs. The modified and abbreviated ETDRS scale per person was derived from a Scale level for either or both eyes, which was used in the ACCORD Eye Study.

The standard retinal digital colour fundus photographs should be taken by the same methodology for a respective person: either 7-field or 4-field-wide photographs and preferably with the same optical device and by the identical eye care professional. Since there are small differences between 7-field and 4-field wide fundus photographs, the applicant was asked to give details on the different modalities for both groups (tirzepatide and dulaglutide) and per person.

## Duration of study intervention

The SURPASS-CVOT DR addendum included 3 study periods:

screening: 2 weeks

dose-escalation period: 24 weeks, and

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

maintenance (follow-up) period: the last scheduled assessment for the SURPASS-CVOT DR addendum was at 36 months (Visit 24).

Participants enrolled in the addendum received additional ophthalmology assessments at baseline, 6, 12, 18, 24, and 36 months.

## Protocol deviations

A total of 396 (43.0%) participants in the addendum had at least 1 important protocol deviation (tirzepatide, 42.8%; dulaglutide, 43.3%).

The most common important protocol deviations in the addendum were related to study procedure compliance (37.0%) of the 340 participants with important protocol deviations in the study procedure compliance category, 104 (30.6%) did not have fundus photographs at Visit 24 (50 in the tirzepatide group and 54 in the dulaglutide group), although the participants continued in the study informed consent (6.0%), and safety reporting (2.0%).

## Demographic and other baseline characteristics:

Mean (SD) age was 64.1 (8.7) years with an even balance between those aged less than 65 (49.0% less than 65 years) and those aged 65 or older (51.0% 65 years or older). 11.0% were 75 years or older.

More men than women were randomised (64.3% were male).

Participants reported the following types of CVD at screening:

coronary artery disease: 57.2%

prior coronary revascularization procedure: 47.8%

prior myocardial infarction: 43.7%

peripheral arterial disease: 31.7%

prior lower extremity arterial revascularization: 4.9%

prior stroke: 18.9%

prior carotid revascularization: 2.2%, and

prior heart failure (at baseline): 23.5%.

Based on fundoscopic exam at baseline, 12.8% of participants had proliferative DR in either eye, and 65.6% had non-proliferative DR with 55.7% of non-proliferative DR cases being mild.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

At the baseline fundoscopic exam, more participants in the tirzepatide group (n=68; 15.2%) than participants in the dulaglutide group (n=50; 12.8%) had proliferative DR in either eye

A high percentage of participants had additional cardiovascular risk factors at screening; 88.8% had hypertension and 81.3% had dyslipidaemia:

mean (SD) SBP: 136.9 (15.3) mm Hg, and

mean (SD) DBP: 77.7 (9.4) mm Hg.

Participants tended to have long-standing diabetes (mean [SD]) duration of T2D was 19.9 [8.3] years). Minimum and maximum duration was 0.1 and 52.1 years, respectively.

A minority of participants had evidence of renal dysfunction at baseline (23.5% of participants had estimated glomerular filtration rate &lt;60 mL/min/1.73 m2, 34.4% had microalbuminuria, and 17.0% had macroalbuminuria).

## DR in participants enrolled in the addendum:

Results for the primary and key secondary objectives of the SURPASS-CVOT DR addendum are presented:

## Primary objective

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The odds ratio of having at least 2 grades worsening of DR per person on the ETDRS Severity Scale (ETDRS2+) at 36 months using an unconditional logistic regression model was 0.89 (tirzepatide versus dulaglutide; 95% CI: 0.59, 1.19). The upper limit of the 95% CI of the odds ratio was less than 1.8 (tirzepatide versus dulaglutide).

## Key secondary objective

The time to onset analysis of ETDRS2+ showed a hazard ratio for participants in the tirzepatide group compared with participants in the dulaglutide group of 0.82 ([95% CI: 0.64, 1.03]; p-value = 0.092).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 28 - Kaplan-Meier plot: time to first onset of ETDRS2+ - Safety dataset in retinopathy addendum

<!-- image -->

Figure 28 presents the Kaplan-Meier plots for time to first onset of ETDRS2+.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Time to first onset of sustained worsening visual acuity of at least 3 lines (15 letters) on either eye

The time to first onset analysis of sustained worsening of visual acuity of at least 3 lines (15 letters) on either eye showed that a hazard ratio for participants in the tirzepatide group compared with participants in the dulaglutide group of 1.18 ([95% CI: 0.82, 1.71]; p-value = 0.379).

Figure 29 - Kaplan-Meier plot: time to first onset of sustained worsening of visual acuity of at least 3 lines (15 letters) on either eye Safety dataset in retinopathy addendum

<!-- image -->

Hazard ratio for both onset of ETDRS2+ (hazard ratio [95% CI]: 0.82 [0.64, 1.03]) and sustained worsening of visual acuity of at least 3 lines (15 letters) on either eye (hazard ratio [95% CI]: 1.18 [0.82, 1.71]) showed no significant differences between treatments.

## Selected Exploratory Endpoints:

The analysis of time to first onset of new diagnosis of ME (based on dilated fundoscopic

examination) showed a hazard ratio for participants in the tirzepatide group compared with participants in the dulaglutide group of 1.15 [95% CI: 0.79, 1.68]; p-value = 0.466).

The analysis of time to first onset of treatment by photocoagulation, intravitreal injections

(anti-VEGF, steroid treatment, or both), vitrectomy, or vitreous haemorrhage showed a hazard ratio for participants in the tirzepatide group of 1.14 [95% CI: 0.76, 1.70]; p-value = 0.524).

The analysis of time to first onset of treatment by pan-retinal photocoagulation showed a hazard ration for participants in the tirzepatide group of 0.37 [95% CI: 0.12, 1.17]; p-value = 0.092).

The analysis of time to first onset of treatment of vitreous haemorrhage showed a hazard ration for participants in the tirzepatide group of 1.66 [95% CI: 0.69, 4.01]; p-value = 0.257).

The analysis of mean change of ETDRS Severity Scale showed a significant increase from baseline in the dulaglutide group at month 24 and 36. There was not a statistically significant difference between the tirzepatide and dulaglutide groups for mean change from baseline of ETDRS Severity Scale at Month 36 (with mixed model repeated measures analysis).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The analysis of mean change of ETDRS Severity Scale by baseline ETDRS (from above) divided in having any retinopathy at baseline showed a significant change for tirzepatide beginning at month 12 and the change from baseline in the dulaglutide group was statistically significant at all post-baseline timepoints. At month 36 (with mixed model repeated measures analysis), the mean change of ETDRS Severity Scale was not significantly different between the tirzepatide and dulaglutide groups for participants with DR or ME at baseline.
