## Depression, suicide or self-injury

The AE listing revealed that the percentage of subjects experiencing at least one AE of depression was balanced between TZP and dulaglutide. Anxiety was numerically more frequent with dulaglutide, see table below.

Table 70 - Summary of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events. MedDRA Preferred Term by Decreasing Total Frequency within System Organ Class. Safety Dataset

| Depression   | 134 (2.0)   | 135 (2.0)   |
|--------------|-------------|-------------|
| Anxiety      | 62 (0.9)    | 88 (1.3)    |

Overall, 14 (0.1%) participants reported at least 1 TEAE of suicide or self-injury SMQ, including

- 5 (0.1%) participants in the tirzepatide group, and
- 9 (0.1%) participants in the dulaglutide group.

Overall, 10 (0.08%) participants experienced 12 severe or serious TEAEs related to suicide or selfinjury, including

- 4 (0.06%) participants in the tirzepatide group, with 5 events, and
- 6 (0.09%) participants in the dulaglutide group, with 7 events.

In spontaneous AE reporting, major depression was found in 6 subjects (0.1%) receiving TZP and in 3 subjects (0.0%) receiving dulaglutide.

## Immunogenicity

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Hypersensitivity reactions

Overall, similar percentages of participants in both treatment groups experienced at least 1 hypersensitivity event (tirzepatide, 377 [5.7%]; dulaglutide, 330 [5.0%]).

The most frequently reported events were:

- Rash (tirzepatide, 65 [1.0%]; dulaglutide, 71 [1.1%])
- Eczema (tirzepatide, 45 [0.7%]; dulaglutide, 35 [0.5%])
- Dermatitis (tirzepatide, 37 [0.6%]; dulaglutide, 31 [0.5%]).

A total of 14 participants (tirzepatide, 9 [0.14%]; dulaglutide, 5 [0.08%]) reported serious or severe events.

## Injection site reactions

The proportion of participants reporting ISRs was similar between the tirzepatide and dulaglutide groups: tirzepatide, 102 (1.5%); dulaglutide, 80 (1.2%).

## Anti-drug antibodies (ADA)

ADA obviously were not investigated in SURPASS-CVOT. This is acceptable since sufficient data from previous studies are available. ADA formation with TZP is common; around half of the treated patients develop ADA. However, no relevant adverse effects related to ADA were observed.

## Laboratory findings

## Haematology

No relevant observations were made.

## Clinical chemistry

Salient findings are covered in the section on ASEs of special interest (AESIs)

## Vital signs

Both groups, dulaglutide and TZP, experienced reductions in systolic blood pressure (SBP) and diastolic blood pressure (DBP) from baseline, especially during dose-escalation period, with tirzepatide demonstrating a greater response throughout the follow-up period.

Pulse rate significantly increased from baseline beginning as early as Week 4 in both treatment groups and reached the maximum value (tirzepatide, 6.1 bpm [Week 20]; dulaglutide, 4.7 bpm [Week 8]) during dose-escalation, then gradually decreased throughout the treatment period (increase ≤3.4 bpm at Month 24 through end of follow-up).

## Discontinuation due to adverse events

AEs leading to discontinuation was highly similar between the treatment groups, dulaglutide and TZP.

## 4.5.1. Discussion on clinical safety

The applicant has provided an extensive safety analysis of this large CV outcome trial SURPASS-CVOT in type 2 diabetes patients. Besides standard collection of adverse events (AEs), a wide array of AEs of special interest (AESIs) was defined and specifically followed. The selection of AESIs was based on potential unfavourable effects of TZP (or GLP-1 RAs in general) that were suspected from previous studies. Overall, the safety profile of TZP observed in SURPASS-CVOT was well in line with the findings from previous studies. For less frequent events such as pancreatitis or cholelithiasis, a more accurate estimate of the incidence could be achieved due to the large size of the study. However, safety conclusions were limited by the fact that the comparator was another GLP-1 RA, dulaglutide, which has similar side effects, albeit with smaller magnitude due to the rather low dose. Nevertheless, with this comparator it is not possible to delineate the full extent of contribution of TZP to a given side effect (e.g. pancreatitis), which also can occur spontaneously. The percentage of subjects experiencing at least one treatment-emergent AE or serious AE was similar for TZP and dulaglutide. The most prominent side effects of TZP were gastrointestinal (GI) events, mainly nausea and vomiting. These effects were most frequent immediately after start of treatment; their incidence decreased after a few months and remained at a lower level thereafter. In accordance with the larger size of the desired effect (HbA1c reduction) of TZP compared to dulaglutide, GI side effects were more frequent and longer lasting with TZP. Other common side effects that were more frequent with TZP than with dulaglutide were fatigue, dizziness and asthenia. Pancreatitis events were adjudicated by an independent committee. The confirmed events of pancreatitis were balanced between TZP and dulaglutide; 0.6% of subjects in each group experienced at least one event. There were in total 46 events in the TZP and 49 events in the dulaglutide group. It is reassuring that events were not more frequent with TZP despite its larger size of desired effects (HbA1c reduction and weight loss). In the dulaglutide CV outcome study REWIND, 0.5% of subjects with dulaglutide had at least one event of adjudicated pancreatitis (27 events) vs. 0.3% (14 events) with plc. Another type of rare AE suspected to be linked with GLP-1 RAs is gallbladder disease. In the present study, a slightly higher percentage of TZP patients than dulaglutide patients experienced a gallbladderrelated event (4.2% TZP vs. 3.6% dulaglutide). This difference was driven by events of cholelithiasis (2.4% TZP vs. 2.0% dulaglutide). Hence, this study confirmed that TZP apparently can cause gall bladder disease in rare cases, mainly cholelithiasis. Due to the low number of events, the difference is not statistically significant. Note that dulaglutide itself can increase the risk of cholelithiasis or other gall bladder disease when compared to placebo. In the CV outcome trial with dulaglutide (REWIND study), 3.2% of subjects treated with dulaglutide had cholelithiasis vs. 2.8% with plc. An important side effect of diabetes therapy is hypoglycaemia. The applicant specifically followed events of severe hypoglycaemia. Based on the mechanism of action, GLP-1 RAs are not expected to cause hypoglycaemia. Accordingly, only 0.2% of subjects in SURPASS-CVOT suffered a severe hypoglycaemia provided they had no accompanying hypoglycaemic therapy (insulin or sulfonylurea [SU]). In subjects with hypoglycaemic background therapy, hypoglycaemia rate was higher, as expected, with no difference between TZP and dulaglutide in most cases. Only in subjects receiving SU and insulin simultaneously hypoglycaemia rate was higher in the TZP than in the dulaglutide group. However, firm conclusions are not possible based on this observation because the number of subjects receiving this (unusual) combination was low. GLP-1 RAs were observed to induce proliferation (hyperplasia, adenoma and carcinoma) of thyroid Ccells in rodents. This effect is accompanied by a large increase in serum calcitonin due to increased production and secretion of calcitonin in the C-cells. TZP is known to slightly increase serum calcitonin in humans. C-cell proliferation was not yet observed in humans. In the present study, two cases of Ccell carcinoma (= medullary thyroid cancer) were observed in the TZP group (none in the dulaglutide group). C-cell carcinomas most often occur in subjects carrying a germline mutation in the RET protooncogen. These subjects were excluded from SURPASS-CVOT. Hence, the number of expected spontaneous cases of medullary thyroid cancer in the TZP group is lower than 2. The applicant pointed out that these two cases probably would not have been detected outside the study, i.e. without a specific screening programme so that comparison with epidemiological data can be misleading. This is agreed. It is also clear that with such low numbers, chance findings cannot be excluded. The applicant plans two registry-based post-marketing studies for further follow-up of the relationship between TZP and medullary thyroid cancer. Therefore, it should be possible in the future to provide a more reliable estimate of MTC incidence with TZP. Regarding malignancies in general, the number of subjects with malignancy was slightly higher in the TZP than in the dulaglutide group, 305 (4.6%) TZP vs. 270 (4.1%) dulaglutide. The largest differences between TZP and dulaglutide were observed for prostate cancer (40 subjects [0.8%] TZP vs. 27 subjects [0.6%] dulaglutide) and endometrial adenocarcinoma (7 [0.4%] vs. 2 [0.1%]). Particularly for endometrium carcinoma, the number of events is low so that firm conclusions are not possible. Both types of cancer, prostate and endometrium, are dependent on sex hormones, but there is no hint that GLP1-RAs mimic sex hormone activity. Indirect effects cannot be excluded; however, it is difficult to draw conclusions on carcinogenicity from even from longer term clinical trials because they are still too short to cover the long period of tumour initiation and development. It was suspected that GLP-1 RAs may favour development of depression, e.g. because their appetitelowering action could deprive susceptible subjects from joy gained by eating. In SURPASS-CVOT, suicide or self-injury were specifically followed. The number of these events was low, and no meaningful difference between TZP and dulaglutide was observed. In spontaneous AE reporting, major depression was rare (6 subjects [0.1%] in the TZP group vs. 3 subjects [0.0%] in the dulaglutide group), indicating that depression is not a relevant side effect of TZP. Another topic of special interest was cardiac arrythmia since this side effect, particularly atrial fibrillation, was suspected in a previous study. SURPASS-CVOT did not reveal evidence that TZP could increase the risk for this condition. The applicant also discussed renal safety as topic of special interest. Simultaneously, the applicant also intended to demonstrate a renal benefit of TZP (see efficacy section). Decline of eGFR over time was followed, in the total study population as well as in suitable subgroups (e.g. subjects with high risk for CKD progression or subjects taking/not taking SGLT2i). The decline was most likely due to the underlying disease (diabetes mell.). In the TZP-treated subjects, eGFR decline was generally slower than in dulaglutide -treated individuals. Thus, there is no concern for renal safety. However, timecourse of eGFR decline in some subgroups markedly depended on the method of GFR estimation (based on serum creatinine vs. serum cystatin C). The applicant explained that GFR estimation based on serum creatinine is not reliable in situations where the muscle mass changes as in case of weight reduction. The pre-defined analyses used the formula that calculates eGFR from both values, serum creatine and serum cystatin C (CDK-EPI Creatinine-Cystatin Equation 2021) as it is recommended in the KDIGO guideline. It is questionable why the primary evaluation included the inappropriate parameter serum creatinine, but evaluations with all equations are available. Thus, the observed phenomena could be explained and no safety concerns remained. During the development of highly effective GLP-1 RAs, it was suspected that they can accelerate the progression of diabetic retinopathy (DR), particularly immediately after start of treatment. This was called early worsening of diabetic retinopathy (EWDR). Therefore, the applicant has extensively addressed this topic in the SURPASS-CVOT study. For the total study population (safety data set), a composite diabetic retinopathy endpoint was defined, and time to first endpoint event was analysed. According to this analysis, TZP had a numerical advantage (HR=0.9) over dulaglutide, i.e. slower progression. The result was not statistically significant. The upper limit of the 95% CI of the HR was

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

1.07 so that at least a larger disadvantage of TZP could be excluded. However, this analysis included all study participants, whereas EWDR risk is particularly high under certain specific conditions, most importantly presence of proliferative DR and rapid HbA1c decrease (1.5 percentage points in 3 months or 2 percentage points in 6 months). The applicant performed respective analyses in the second round of assessment, and reassuringly it turned out that even in patients with proliferative DR at baseline TZP was numerically better than dulaglutide (HR 0.88, not statistically significant). Furthermore, the Kaplan-Meier plot gave no hint for any unfavourable early effects of TZP.

The SURPASS-CVOT diabetic retinopathy (DR) addendum aimed to compare the effect of tirzepatide maximum tolerated dose (up to 15 mg once weekly) with dulaglutide (1.5 mg once-weekly) on diabetic retinopathy (DR) progression. Patients with DR or macular oedema (ME) or patients without DR or ME but high-risk for developing DR were included. These patients received additional ophthalmology assessments (periodic fundus photographs, visual acuity testing and dilated fundoscopic exams) at baseline and during the addendum maintenance period. Most baseline characteristics were even balanced between the two groups.

The applicant used a modified and abbreviated version of the Early Treatment Diabetic Retinopathy (ETDRS) Diabetic Retinopathy Severity Score (DRSS) for recording retinopathy status. Digital colour fundus photographs were taken after dilated fundoscopic exam and evaluated centrally and adjudicated according to the ETDR DRSS, which is endorsed. A change of at least 2 grades was defined as clinically relevant for the primary endpoint, which is acceptable.

The primary endpoint was analysed using a logistic regression analysis with treatment, baseline sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 inhibitor use (Yes or No), baseline haemoglobin A1c (HbA1c), and duration of type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2D) as fixed effects, and baseline value of Early Treatment Diabetic Retinopathy Study (ETDRS) scale as a covariate.

The odds ratio of having at least 2 grades worsening of DR per person on the ETDRS Severity Scale (ETDRS2+) at 36 months using an unconditional logistic regression model was 0.89 (tirzepatide versus dulaglutide; 95% CI: 0.59, 1.19; rates of progression: 23.5% with TZP, 25% with dulaglutide). The upper limit of the 95% CI of the odds ratio was less than 1.8 (tirzepatide versus dulaglutide) which was the pre-defined upper margin for non-inferiority. This allows to interpret non-inferiority of tirzepatide versus dulaglutide in terms of DR progression under medication at month 36.

Albeit the non-inferiority margin, set to 1.8, allowed for a rather large relative worsening of DR with tirzepatide compared to dulaglutide, this issue is considered without relevance, as the actual upper bound of the 95% CI was 1.19. Even in the event a stricter non-inferiority margin had been set, this would not have altered conclusions.

In addition, participants were categorized based on the HbA1c reduction observed at week 24 and their ETDRS2+ results at month 36. No clear correlation of the ETDRS results at month 36 to initial HbA1c changes was shown. These results at week 36 are of relevance to assess whether early glycaemic improvement predicts sustained long-term retinal outcomes, as some morphological changes may develop over extended periods. Upon request, the Applicant performed a correlation analysis between HbA1c change at Week 24 to retinal changes at Week 24. Results pointed to no significant relationship between initial lowering of HbA1c and ETDRS severity score change at week 24 (correlation coefficients near 0). These findings do not support an early worsening phenomenon associated with rapid HbA1c reduction in the first 24 weeks of treatment. However, it may be questioned whether the ETDRS is a sensitive tool to detect changes after 24 weeks, as not all single components of this score are expected to change immediately in the wake of glycaemic improvement.

Overall, the phenomenon of early worsening of retinopathy due to rapid initial glucose lowering described with GLP1-agonists was not confirmed with TZP and dulaglutide in the diabetic retinopathy substudy. This may be partly due to the insensitivity of the ETDRS severity score (or its individual components) to capture early retinal changes. In line with the findings of this substudy, analyses of combined DR endpoints presented for the overall SURPASS-CVOT population did also not indicate a more rapid disease progression with TZP in the early treatment phase. Overall, data cannot invalidate prior experience with GLP-1 receptor agonists which showed early worsening to DR probably associated to fast initial glucose lowering. To make the prescriber aware of this, a general warning statement is included into the SmPC (Section 4.4).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Key secondary endpoints were time-to-event endpoints, based on Cox proportional hazard model stratified by sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 inhibitor use (Yes or No) at baseline, with treatment, baseline HbA1c, and duration of T2D as fixed effects, and baseline value as a covariate. Participants who received DR treatment during the follow-up period were considered to have experienced an event on the date of DR treatment.

The time to onset analysis of ETDRS2+ showed a hazard ratio for participants in the tirzepatide group compared with participants in the dulaglutide group of 0.82 ([95% CI: 0.64, 1.03]; p-value = 0.092).

The time to first onset analysis of sustained worsening of visual acuity of at least 3 lines (15 letters) on either eye showed that a hazard ratio for participants in the tirzepatide group compared with participants in the dulaglutide group of 1.18 ([95% CI: 0.82, 1.71]; p-value = 0.379).

Both results indicate non-inferiority for tirzepatide versus dulaglutide in terms of time to onset of DR progression and in terms of visual loss on either eye under medication after 36 months.

Dulaglutide is the active comparator to tirzepatide in this study and according to the primary endpoint, tirzepatide is non-inferior to dulaglutide in terms of DR progression after 36months. In the REWINDstudy, where dulaglutide is compared to placebo, dulaglutide appeared to be not associated with an (markedly) increased incidence (1.9%) of treatments of retinopathy complications compared with placebo (1.5%, p=0.16). A non-inferiority approach comparing tirzepatide vs dulaglutide may therefore be justified.

Immunogenicity, particularly formation of anti-drug antibodies (ADA), was not addressed in SURPASSCVOT. This is acceptable because previous studies with TZP provided an unambiguous picture. Around half of the subjects treated with TZP usually develop ADA, which, however, have no relevant clinical consequences; they merely may somewhat increase the risk of injection site reactions. Injection site reactions and systemic hypersensitivity reactions can be derived from the AE recording in SURPASSCVOT. The percentage of subjects experiencing injection site reactions was slightly increased with TZP compared to dulaglutide (1.5% vs. 1.2%). This is not considered a safety concern. There was no hint for increased risk of systemic hypersensitivity reactions with TZP.

TZP and other GLP-1 RAs are known to affect vital signs, particularly an increase in heart rate. This observation was also made in SURPASS-CVOT, the effect being larger with TZP than with dulaglutide. Furthermore, TZP (and dulaglutide to a lesser extent) decreased systolic and diastolic blood pressure.

## 4.5.2. Conclusions on clinical safety

Overall, SURPASS-CVOT did not reveal new safety concerns of TZP. A general warning is inserted in section 4.4 of the SmPC to make the prescriber aware of a potential risk of worsening of diabetic retinopathy in the wake of early glycaemic improvement.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.5.3. PSUR cycle

The requirements for submission of periodic safety update reports for this medicinal product are set out in the list of Union reference dates (EURD list) provided for under Article 107c(7) of Directive 2001/83/EC and any subsequent updates published on the European medicines web-portal.

## 4.6. Risk management plan

The MAH has submitted an updated RMP; version 10.1. The proposed updates in the RMP are related to Study I8F-MC-GPGN (SURPASS-CVOT). The updates include incorporation of data from the Study I8F-MC-GPGN into the epidemiology, clinical trial, and post-marketing exposure sections. These updates are acceptable.

The MAH proposed the removal of diabetic retinopathy complications as an important potential risk from the list of safety concerns in the RMP. This is accepted. However, the important potential risk of diabetic retinopathy complications should continue to be monitored and discussed in the PSUR, and therefore should remain included in the list of safety concerns in the PSUR.

The MAH proposed to update the status of study I8F-MC-GPGN from an ongoing additional pharmacovigilance activity to a completed study, which is acceptable.

## 4.6.1. Overall conclusion on the RMP

RMP version 10.1 is considered acceptable.

## 4.7. Changes to the Product Information

As a result of this variation, sections 4.1, 4.4, 4.8, and 5.1 of the SmPC have been updated, together with the package leaflet, where applicable.

## 4.7.1. User consultation

A justification for not performing a full user consultation with target patient groups on the package leaflet has been submitted by the MAH and has been found acceptable. The justification is summarised in the following:

User testing has been performed and accepted by CHMP on the package leaflet for Mounjaro solution for injection in prefilled pen, as part of the initial marketing authorisation application (MAA) concerning the following approved indication:

\"Mounjaro is indicated for the treatment of adults with insufficiently controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus as an adjunct to diet and exercise - as monotherapy when metformin is considered inappropriate due to intolerance or contraindications - in addition to other medicinal products for the treatment of diabetes.\"

The Mounjaro package leaflet met the pass criteria for readability testing for the above initial application.

The Mounjaro package leaflet is now being updated due to the addition of the new indication to reduce major adverse cardiovascular events in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus and established cardiovascular disease, the subject of this type II variation. The expected text modifications to the package leaflet resulting from this variation are minor and do not include text that is significantly different from that already user tested. Overall, the structure and design of the revised Mounjaro Package Leaflet is not expected to be changed significantly due to the new information and the revisions are unlikely to affect the overall readability.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.8. Benefit-Risk Balance

## 4.8.1. Therapeutic Context

Initially, with this type II variation, the following indication (section 4.1 SmPC) was proposed to be added:

Mounjaro is indicated to reduce the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction, or stroke) in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus and established cardiovascular disease.

This new claim has been based on the submitted cardiovascular outcome study SURPASS-CVOT.

Tirzepatide is a long-acting glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide (GIP) receptor and glucagonlike peptide-1 (GLP-1) receptor agonist (RA). Tirzepatide selectively binds to and activates both the GIP and GLP-1 receptors, the targets for native GIP and GLP-1.

Tirzepatide was first authorized to be marketed on 13 May 2022 in the United States and since has been approved for one or more of the following indications in more than 65 countries, including in the EU, Canada, Australia, Japan, Switzerland, and China, with applications either approved or under review in other regions:

-T2D in adults to improve glycaemic control

- weight management in adults with obesity, or overweight with weight-related comorbidities, and
- moderate-to-severe obstructive sleep apnoea in adults with obesity.

## 4.8.1.1. Disease or condition

T2D is a metabolic condition characterized by impaired glycaemic control resulting from increased insulin resistance and progressive beta-cell dysfunction, leading to insufficient insulin secretion. According to the International Diabetes Federation (IDF 2025), an estimated 589 million adults globally are currently living with diabetes, a number projected to rise to 853 million by 2050. T2D is commonly associated with comorbidities like increased weight or obesity, hypertension, and dyslipidaemia, which contribute to an elevated risk of both macrovascular and microvascular complications. T2D is responsible for an average 2-fold higher risk for CV events, including coronary heart disease, ischemic stroke, HFE, and vascular death, independent of other established risk factors (Rawshani et al. 2018; Martin et al. 2025). Approximately 90% of patients with T2D are overweight or obese, and excess weight plays a central role in the development of insulin resistance and related cardiometabolic complications. CVD, particularly ASCVD, remains the leading cause of morbidity and mortality in people with diabetes, especially those with T2D (Rawshani et al. 2017). This elevated risk is largely attributable to the frequent clustering of major CV risk factors in T2D, including hypertension, dyslipidaemia, and obesity.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.8.1.2. Available therapies and unmet medical need

Current international guidelines emphasize the use of SGLT2is and GLP-1 RAs in patients with T2D who have ASCVD, high CV risk, CKD, or HF (Davies et al. 2022). Furthermore, both SGLT2is and GLP-1 RAs have been shown to improve long-term outcomes in patients with T2D (ADA 2025).

Treatment guidelines for T2D encourage the use of antihyperglycaemic agents with proven CV benefits as second-line therapy for the treatment of diabetes in patients with established CVD. The available agents recommended for reducing the risk of MACE in people with T2D and ASCVD currently include SGLT2i (empagliflozin, dapagliflozin and canagliflozin) and GLP-1 RA (liraglutide, dulaglutide, and semaglutide), all of which showed CV benefit in a patient population with T2D and primarily established CVD. This benefit is reflected in the respective SmPCs. Hence, there is no unmet medical need for an additional antihyperglycaemic, cardioprotective agent.

## 4.8.1.3. Main clinical studies

SURPASS-CVOT was a phase 3, event-driven, multicenter, randomised, double-blind, active comparator, parallel-group study. The study assessed the efficacy of once weekly tirzepatide maximal tolerated dose on MACE-3 incidence, compared with once weekly dulaglutide 1.5 mg, when added to standard of care in participants with T2D with established CVD. Study participants were randomly assigned 1:1 to tirzepatide or dulaglutide and were stratified by country of enrolment and baseline SGLT-2i use status.

CV outcomes were assessed as they occurred, and other measures were collected every 4 weeks for the first 6 months and then followed approximately every 3 months thereafter.

## Methodology

Generally, efficacy analyses were based on the modified intention-to-treat (mITT) dataset, which includes data from all randomised participants collected during the follow-up period, regardless of study drug status. Participants who were randomised in error were excluded from the mITT dataset, which is considered justified, as the decision was made before unblinding and the same criteria were applied to both treatment arms.

Although no estimand was explicitly defined, the primary analysis of the primary endpoint based on the mITT dataset is considered to align with the treatment policy strategy for handling intercurrent events related to study drug status, such as treatment interruptions or discontinuations. This approach reflects real-world clinical practice and is regarded relevant for clinical decision-making. In the context of the non-inferiority test, the frequency and distribution of intercurrent events across the two treatment arms are critical for the proper interpretation of the analysis results.

Sensitivity analyses, including multiple imputation of missing follow-up time, tipping point analyses, and an 'on-treatment analysis' were planned to further evaluate the robustness of the conclusions of the primary analysis. These approaches are supported. For the determination of the non-inferiority margin for the hazard ratio of tirzepatide versus dulaglutide regarding the primary endpoint, a Bayesian hierarchical meta-analysis was performed using the secondary prevention cohort from the REWIND CVOT and secondary prevention cohorts from five other GLP-1 RA CVOTs to estimate the effect of dulaglutide versus placebo. An NI margin of 1.05 for comparing tirzepatide and dulaglutide is chosen, which represents the inverse of the 97.5th percentile of the posterior distribution of HR (dulaglutide versus placebo) and ensures preservation of at least 50% of the dulaglutide efficacy if the observed HR (tirzepatide versus dulaglutide) &lt;0.95. The chosen NI margin is acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A graphical approach was pre-specified to control the type I error for the analysis of the primary and key secondary endpoints, taking into account one interim analysis for efficacy. This approach is considered acceptable.

A SAP prespecified indirect comparison of tirzepatide versus placebo was conducted for the primary and CV-related key secondary endpoints utilizing individual participant data from SURPASS-CVOT and REWIND. The analysis included data from REWIND participants who would have been eligible to enroll in SURPASS-CVOT, thus representing a comparable cohort with SURPASS-CVOT, and all SURPASSCVOT participants, and estimated the effect of tirzepatide to reduce the CV risk compared with an indirect placebo. In addition, an indirect comparison sensitivity analysis was conducted utilizing all REWIND participants. For both analyses, the indirect treatment effect was derived by multiplying the HR for SURPASS-CVOT comparing tirzepatide versus dulaglutide by the HR for REWIND comparing dulaglutide versus placebo. HRs were adjusted based on stabilized inverse-probability weight calculated based on the estimated propensity score. Due to the potential bias inherent in the indirect comparison, the results can only be considered supportive, not definitive.

## Study population

SURPASS-CVOT was a global study that enrolled 13,299 participants with T2D and established CVD from 30 countries. Key inclusion criteria were established CVD disease (coronary artery disease, cerebrovascular disease, peripheral arterial disease), HbA1c between 7% and 10.5%, and a BMI of at least 25 kg/m². The in-and exclusion criteria are considered adequate to identify a T2D population at higher risk for cardiovascular events.

Included patients had a mean age of 64.1 years and the mean BMI of 32.6 kg/m 2 . The mean duration of T2D was 14.7 years, the mean eGFR was 76.5 mL/min per 1.73 m2, and the mean percentage of HbA1c was 8.4%; 57.2% had a coronary revascularization, 47.2% had prior MI, 20.3% had HF, and 19.2% had prior stroke. The percentage of participants with a high or very-high risk CKD as defined by the KDIGO guideline (2024) was 22.2%.

## Objectives and endpoints

The primary endpoint was MACE-3 Secondary (and exploratory) endpoints investigated components of the cardiovascular composite, a MACE-4 endpoint including revascularisation, a time to event analysis for hospitalisation for heart failure/urgent heart failure visit, hospitalisation for unstable angina as well as cardiometabolic endpoints (HbA1c, blood lipids, body weight, waist circumference, blood pressure). In addition, endpoints exploring the evolution of renal function were defined as secondary endpoints and evaluated in the mITT population as well as in the subset of participants with high- or very-highrisk CKD (KDIGO criteria, 2025). Patient reported outcome was also captured by the application of three questionnaires.

## 4.8.2. Favourable effects

The primary composite outcome occurred in 801 (12.2%) participants (3.25 events per 100 PY of follow-up) in the tirzepatide group and 862 (13.1%) participants (3.54 events per 100 PY of follow-up) in the dulaglutide group. Noninferiority of MACE-3 was confirmed for tirzepatide compared to dulaglutide with a HR of 0.92 (95.3% CI: 0.83, 1.01; noninferiority p-value = 0.007). The upper bound of the CI (1.01) was less than the prespecified noninferiority margin of 1.05.

The effect was consistent across the three components of MACE; the HR (95% CI) were 0.89 (0.77, 1.02) for death from CV or undetermined causes, 0.86 (0.74, 1.00) for MI, and 0.91 (0.76, 1.09) for stroke.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Since superiority of tirzepatide versus dulaglutide was not established, formal statistical comparison between treatment groups of key secondary endpoints was not controlled for family-wise Type 1 error rate. Although 95% CIs and p-values are provided in the effects table to describe the results, these pvalues are nominal.

The incidences of all-cause death (HR 0.84, CI 0.75, 0.94), time to CV death (HR 0.89, CI 0.77, 1.02), time to first occurrence of CV death or HFE (HR 0.91, CI 0.81, 1.03), time to first occurrence of MACE4 (HR 0.88, CI 0.81, 0.96) tended to be lower in the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group. The difference in deaths was most pronounced for non-cardiovascular deaths; infections caused 1.2% of non-cardiovascular deaths in the tirzepatide group and 2.1% of non-cardiovascular deaths in the dulaglutide group.

The decline in eGFR in the subset of patients at risk of CKD progression was nominally significantly reduced in the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group at Month 36 (difference between treatment groups 3.17 mL/min/1.73 m 2 , 95% CI: 2.09, 4.26). This result was supported by the slope analysis which showed that the annual rate of decline in eGFR for participants with high risk for CKD progression tended to be nominally slower for participants in the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group (0.93 mL/min/1.73m 2 per year, 95% CI: 0.65, 1.22, p-value &lt;0.001).

The incidence of a renal outcome events (composite renal endpoint 2: persistent ≥40% reduction in eGFR, ESRD, or renal death; defined as additional secondary endpoint) were less frequent with TZP compared to dulaglutide. In the renal high-risk population TZP led to a nominally statistically significant reduction in renal outcome events compared to dulaglutide.

Overall, TZP led to an additional reduction in HbA1c compared to dulaglutide as well as to a reduction in body weight and waist circumference. There were slight improvements in systolic blood pressure and lipid profile. The difference between treatment groups on reduction of SBP (-2.1 mmHg) is considered clinically relevant. Except for LDL cholesterol, TZP led to numerically greater improvements in lipid parameters compared to dulaglutide.

## 4.8.3. Uncertainties and limitations about favourable effects

As the changes in the PROs questionnaires were small and mostly below the minimally clinically relevant difference, weight loss and improved glycaemic control did not translate into the patient`s perceived well-being.

The restriction of the SURPASS-CVOT data to patients with T2DM can be seen as a general limitation.

## 4.8.4. Unfavourable effects

In SURPASS-CVOT, TZP revealed the established safety profile. The most prominent side effects were related to the gastrointestinal (GI) tract, mainly, nausea, vomiting and diarrhoea. Pancreatitis, a rare but serous side effect potentially linked to GLP-1 RAs, was not increased with TZP compared to control in SURPASS-CVOT. This may be related to the fact that the comparator (dulaglutide) is also a GLP-1 RA. Reassuringly, pancreatitis incidence with TZP was not higher than with dulaglutide despite the larger effect size of TZP in respect to desired effects (HbA1c reduction and weight reduction). Gall bladder disease, particularly cholelithiasis, another suspected (rare) side effect of GLP-1 RAs, was numerically more frequent with TZP than with dulaglutide. As expected from experience with previous studies, TZP increased the serum levels of pancreas amylase. lipase and calcitonin. No side effects of TZP that were not described or suspected previously became obvious in SURPASS-CVOT.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.8.5. Uncertainties and limitations about unfavourable effects

The safety analysis of TZP in SURPASS-CVOT is limited by the fact that the comparator was also a GLP1 RA and therefore had a similar safety profile. Hence, undesired effects related to TZP cannot be fully recognised because they also may occur in the comparator group. However, often the frequency of GLP-1 RA-related AEs was higher with TZP than with comparator dulaglutide because of the rather low dosage of the latter.

A potential safety concern of GLP-1 RAs is the formation of thyroid C-cell tumours, i.e. medullary carcinoma. This is based on observations in rodents where GLP-1 RAs cause strong production and secretion of calcitonin in the C-cells, leading to C-cell hyperplasia and, later, cell adenomas and carcinomas. In humans, C-cell tumours are rare and most often occur in subjects carrying a RET mutation. These subjects usually are excluded from GLP-1 RA studies so that medullary thyroid carcinomas are rarely observed in these studies. However, two cases of medullary cancer were reported in the TZP group of SURPASS-CVOT. These

Non-CV mortality in SURPASS-CVOT was to a marked extent due to fatal infections (mainly pneumonia and sepsis). These events were more frequent with dulaglutide than with TZP. The reason is unclear. This probably was due to the poorer glycaemic control and lower weight reduction with dulaglutide, which in turn can favour infections. When comparing dulaglutide to plc (in the dulaglutide CV outcome trial REWIND), the rate of infections, including fatal ones, was higher with plc, in line with the poorer glycaemic control in the plc group.

Although the applicant intended to demonstrate beneficial effects of TZP in respect to progression of diabetic kidney disease, the applicant provided an extensive safety analysis of eGFR development over time. In certain subgroups, eGFR markedly increased after start of TZP treatment, in particular in analyses in which eGFR was calculated from serum creatinine. The latter is inappropriate in subjects with rapidly changing muscle mass as in case of weight loss. When calculating eGFR from serum cystatin C, no unexplained findings remained.

It is suspected that newer GLP-1 RAs can accelerate the progression of diabetic retinopathy (DR), particularly immediately after start of treatment. This was called early worsening of diabetic retinopathy (EWDR). Published literature suggests that EWDR particularly occurs in subjects with proliferative DR and when HbA1c decrease is rapid. The applicant provided a Kaplan-Meier analysis of subjects with proliferative DR at baseline. Reassuringly, also in this vulnerable subgroup TZP was numerically slightly superior to dulaglutide in respect to DR events. Furthermore, the KM plot gave no hints for any unfavourable early effects of TZP compared to dulaglutide.

Uncertainties with regard to the retinopathy substudy concern methodological issues, which the main concern that early worsening of DR in the wake of HbA1c reduction has been insufficiently captured. A general warning is included in section 4.4 of the SmPC in line with the wording of other GLP1-agonists.

## 4.8.7. Benefit-risk assessment and discussion

## 4.8.7.1. Importance of favourable and unfavourable effects

The most important favourable effect of TZP is demonstration of non-inferiority as regards the primary composite outcome which occurred in 801 (12.2%) participants (3.25 events per 100 PY of follow-up) in the tirzepatide group and 862 (13.1%) participants (3.54 events per 100 PY of follow-up) in the dulaglutide group. Consistently, all three components of the MACE-3 endpoints were reduced to a greater extent with TZP compared to dulaglutide. Reduction of macrovascular events in patients with T2DM with high cardiovascular risk is of clinical relevance. Other important findings were the lower incidences of all-cause death (HR 0.84, CI 0.75, 0.94), time to CV death (HR 0.89, CI 0.77, 1.02), time to first occurrence of CV death or HFE (HR 0.91, CI 0.81, 1.03), time to first occurrence of MACE-4 (HR 0.88, CI 0.81, 0.96) with TZP compared to dulaglutide. Reduction of macrovascular events in patients with T2DM with high cardiovascular risk is clearly of clinical relevance.

The decline in eGFR in the subset of patients at risk of CKD progression which was nominally significantly reduced in the tirzepatide group compared with the dulaglutide group at Month 36 (difference between treatment groups 3.17 mL/min/1.73 m2, 95% CI: 2.09, 4.26), a result supported by a less steep eGFR slope with TZP compared to dulaglutide, could also be seen as an important favourable effect. This benefit is adequately reflected in section 5.1 of the SmPC.

Other important favourable effects, which can be regarded the cause for the macrovascular benefit, were improvement of glycaemic control, improvement of hypertension, and weight loss.

## Importance of unfavourable effects:

The SURPASS-CVOT study did not reveal new safety concerns. The main and most frequent unfavourable effects of TZP are related to the GI tract. They mainly occur at treatment start and can be controlled by dose reduction or stopping dose-uptitration before reaching the maximal dose. TZP effects on pancreas (pancreatitis) and gall bladder (mainly cholelithiasis) are more serious but rare. They are listed in the SmPC so that user and prescriber are aware of these possible side effects which allows early diagnosis and treatment. Another important potential side effect is early worsening of diabetic retinopathy. In patients who are at risk of this condition, slow HbA1c reduction could be useful.

## 4.8.7.2. Balance of benefits and risks

In SURPASS-CVOT, TZP showed a reduction of MACE-3 events that was non inferior to the effect of dulaglutide. The latter had shown CV benefit in a prior CVOT (REWIND study). Results of key secondary endpoints and results in subgroups were consistent.

The B-R balance is positive.

## 4.8.7.3. Additional considerations on the benefit-risk balance

## Therapeutic indication

The MAH submitted a variation application under category C.6 of the variation classification Guideline with the scope to include:

\"Extension of indication to reduce the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction, or stroke) in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus and established cardiovascular disease for MOUNJARO, based on final results from study I8F-MC-GPGN (SURPASSCVOT). SURPASS-CVOT was a Phase 3, event-driven, multicentre, international, randomised, doubleblind, active-comparator, parallel-group study to assess the effect of tirzepatide versus dulaglutide on major adverse cardiovascular events in participants with type 2 diabetes. As a consequence, sections 4.1, 4.4, 4.8 and 5.1 of the SmPC are updated. The Package Leaflet is updated in accordance. Version 8.1 of the RMP has also been submitted. In addition, the MAH took the opportunity to introduce minor editorial and formatting changes to the PI.\"

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Based on the assessment of the data contained in the application, the CHMP is of the view that the following changes to the MA should be introduced:

\"Update of sections 4.1, 4.4, 4.8 and 5.1 of the SmPC based on final results from study I8F-MC-GPGN (SURPASS-CVOT). SURPASS-CVOT was a Phase 3, event-driven, multicentre, international, randomised, double-blind, active-comparator, parallel-group study to assess the effect of tirzepatide versus dulaglutide on major adverse cardiovascular events in participants with type 2 diabetes. As a consequence, sections 4.1, 4.4, 4.8 and 5.1 of the SmPC are updated. The Package Leaflet is updated in accordance. Version 8.1 of the RMP has also been submitted. In addition, the MAH took the opportunity to introduce minor editorial and formatting changes to the PI.\"

These changes fall under category C.4 of the variation classification Guideline.

In reply to a Request for Supplementary Information during the procedure, the MAH updated the product information accordingly.

The indication proposed by the Applicant: \"Mounjaro is indicated to reduce the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction, or stroke) in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus and established cardiovascular disease.\" was found as not acceptable albeit efficacy as regards cardioprotection has been demonstrated. This is due to the CHMP policy which views cardiovascular outcome events (myocardial infarction, stroke, cardiovascular death) as important health outcomes associated with T2DM. As such, a crucial aim of antihyperglycaemic therapy is to prevent these events. In the submitted study these adverse outcomes were reduced with TZP to a numerically greater extent compared to dulaglutide in T2DM patients with higher cardiovascular risk. Hence, the beneficial effect of TZP is already sufficiently covered by the current T2DM indication. With the Response the Applicant deleted the a. m. wording from section 4.1. and aligned the wording with section 4.1 of other GLP-1 RAs.

SURPASS-CVOT results are now reflected in section 4.1 as follows (new text in bold):

\"For study results with respect to combinations, effects on glycaemic control, the populations studied, and cardiovascular events see sections 4.4, 4.5 and 5.1.\"

## 4.8.8. Conclusions

The overall B/R of Mounjaro is positive.
